CARETTEB " FOR LONDON.

It is said that the next fort stormed by the Suffragettes will be that portion of the Scotland Yard Bastille where taximeter drivers aro judged and licensed. Paris has her cabwomen, and, in spite of the good-natured ridicule heaped on them at first, they are apparently increasing in number. If Paris leads the way, asks the Sketch, should London wait? -2-so observe the Suffragettes who desire to be Jehus; and when one thinks of the number of ladies k who own and drive motor-cars, one is tempted to say why, in- deed? A very long time ago, when hansom-cabs were first invented and when it was considered decidedly "fast" for a woman to be seen alone in one of those fascinating vehicles, it was whis- pered that a well-known lady whip, and a Peer- ess to boot, had dressed up in a suit of her dimi- nutive husband's clothes and driven a hansom down Piccadilly for a bet. We may yet see "cabettee" established on the principal "taxi" ranks, and it will be interesting to see if there really exists an, law against a woman driving a publio vehicle licensed for hire.

===== 0002601_19070824_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
EMPIRE DRESSING-GOWN.

In our sketch is shewn a simple design for an Empire dressing- or morning-gown. It could be carried out in any light woollen material such as fine cashmere, or it would look particularly well in mauve - and - white patterned delaine. The short Empire bodice has a little fulness in front, which is gathered into a pointed belt of mauve

silk. The gown fastens on the left shoulder, and is trimmed with bands of white Cluny lace, bor- dered with a narrow strap of mauve silk. The trimmirr, meets in a point at the centre of the back, and at the left side is continued down the Side of the skirt to the hem. A band of lace, edged with silk, outlines the square neck, and the loose-flowing sleeves are arranged with a small puff at the top of the arm, drawn in with a band of the lace trimming.

A NOVEL CHATELAINE.

A new idea in the —ch.aierai;;--line, and one which has taken on with travellers, especially yachtswomen, is a huge gold walnut, which opens with a spring, and discloses a little mirror, some powder, a tiny stick of rouge, or other odds and ends; a chain is attached to the nut, and it fastens to the belt. I saw one, says a. writer in P. T. 0., worn with a yachting cos- tume; it looked completely out of place and out of keeping. Such an extravagant costume as it was, too! White linen skirt. with bands of black . lace. The sailor blouse had a large collar of black tussore, and the belt was of black suede; • the collar was cut rather low, and the neck was bare. There was a bright gold buckle to the , belt, and the large gold nut was suspended from a gold bar. Altogether there was only one re- deeming feature about such a get-up—its wearer was a German. TO-DAY'S RECIPE. ! EGGS AU GRATIN. One tablespoonful of : breadcrumbs. 3oz. of butter, three anchovies (chopped), one teaspoonful of finely-chopped parsley, the yolks of two eggs, pepper. salt, and grated nutmeg to taste, and four whole eggs. Put 2oz. of the butter into a basin, and with a wooden spoon beat it to a cream. Add the brcadcrumbs. anchovies, parsley, the two egg • yolks, and the seasoning, and rub all well to- gether. Butter a fireproof dish with the re- mainder of the butter, and then spread the mix- ture at the bottom, and place the dish over a mild heat for two minutes. Then break the four whole eggs carefully on to the top of the mix- ture, and cook for five minutes in a moderato Ivor.. Ser., very i-vot tien making omelettes, care should be taken to whisk the eggs very thoroughly; to have a br)ght. clear fire, not too hot; to serve the ome- lette immediately it is cooked; and always to keep a pan for the cooking of omelettes only. This pan should not be washed, but should be cleaned by rubbing it with a little warm butter anti dried with a soft cloth.

===== 0002601_19070824_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King's Highland Holiday.—The King is expected in the Highlands about the middle of September, and his Majesty's holiday in,Scotland is to include a visit to Mr. and Mrs. Arthur Sassoon at Tulchan Lodge, Strathspey. Tulchan, which Mr. Sassoon rents from the Dowager Lady Seafield, not farfrom Grantown, and not only provides some of the best shooting and fishing, but is also situated amidst some of the grandest scenery in the Highlands. Mrs. Arthur Sassoon is use her sister , Mrs. Leopold Rothschild, very popular in Society, and is one of the most notable hostesses on Speyside. She I is a very good bridge player, an accomplishment the King appreciates, as his Majesty is very partial to bridge, the stakes being usually shilling points. With the command of unlimited wealth, Mrs. Sassoon's entertainments are arranged accordingly, and invitations to join the house party at Tulchan are mach sought after as the place is both lusuriantlyappointed, and is a veritable sportsman's paradise. A Marchioness on Deer-Stalking.—Lady Breadal- bane's 'book on deer-stalking, entitled "The High Tops of Black Mount "published by Messrs. W. Blackwood and Sons, appears at an opportune time, when sports- men and sportswomen are hastening north to enjoy the invigorating mountain air, and the perfume of the purple heather. Lady Breadalbane tells some good stories characteristic of the dour Highland ghillies and her book is illustrated by reproductions of some wonderfully realistic photographs. The author is one of the best rifle shots in the Kingdom, a lover of Nature, and of country life and its sports. It has been said that the fox likes being hunted. Reynard prob- ably has his own opinions on the subject, and it seems never to have occurred to Lady Breadalbane that deer- stalking may be less fun to the deer than to the stalker. She shares to the full the zest for sport. Her ambition is to kill six step with six shots, and it is rather a dis- appointing day if a wounded deer should manage to get away. The New Duke and Duchess of Pless.—The late Duke of Pleas is succeeded by Prince Henry of Pless, who is well-known in London Society. His wife is a daughter of Colonel and Mrs. Cornwallis West, and a sister of the Duchess of Westminster. She was famous before her marriage as one of the most beautiful girls in Society, and became engaged at the end of her first season to Prince Henry. She was married in 1891, and her son, born in 1900, is now heir to the title and vast estates, which are valued at something like three or four millions sterling. The late Duke insisted on the Prince and Princess spending part of every year at Furstenstein, on the borders of Pavaria where the Dukes of Pleas live in almost feudal state. /rhe life was not exactly Suited to the tastes of an English girl, and since their marriage Prince and Princess Henry have spent much of their time in this country. Mr. and Mrs. Willie James.—The bilth of a son to Mr. and Mrs. Willie James, has brought endless con- gratulations to the happy pair, first and foremost those of the King and Queen, and the Prince and Princess of Wales—whose friendship they have long enjoyed, Mrs. Willie James, nee Miss Evelyn Forbes, is a great favourite with many members of the Royal Family, and she has frequently bad the honour of entertaining Royalty at West Dean Park, near Chichester. This house, together with the estate of 8,000 acres, was pur- chased many years ago by Mr. Willie James, and the extensive and finely wooded park runs to within a short distance of Goodwood racecourse. Splendidly decorated and furnished, West Dean is one of the most delightful among country houses, and invitations to the parties so frequently gathered together there, are much sought after. Mrs. Willie James is slight, short, with a pretty winning face, and despite her terrible shortsightedness she possesses the gayest spirits that are seldom, if ever, shadowed. Mr. and Mrs. Willie James were married in 1889, and have three daughters, to whom they are devoted. __ _ _ _

Famous Hymn Writers.—The Rev. Canon Hervey, who has resigned the living of Sandringham in conse- quence of ill-health, is the author of several well-known hymns, but it is a fact that many of our favourite hymns, and notably those sung at times of public mourning, have been written by women. Frances Ridley Havergal, the Countess of Huntingdon, and Charlotte Elliott, have given us many of our finest hymns. "Nearer my God to Thee" was written by Sarah Adams and "Safe in the arms of Jesus," was the work of Fanny Crosby, who was blind from childhood. One of the best known of Canon Harvey's hymns is "The roses hues of early dawn." He is a nephenr of the late Marquis of Bristol, and his wife is related to the Duke of Ricbmond. His resignation is much regretted by the King and Queen, before whom he has officiated on innumerable occasions, he having been Rector of the consolidated parishes of Sandringham, West Newton, and Babingley for close upon thirty years. Women and Clerical Work.—The Society of Arts Intermediate Examinations once more illustrate the triumphant progress of women in out-distancing men in the chief branches of clerical work. In shorthand and typewriting, they took all the money prizes, and headed the lists of medallists in both subjects, as well as for French and German. The only subject in which they did not completely out-distance their male competitors is book-keeping, but in this, as in other branches, it is more than probable that Meg will before long wrest the supremacy from them. Now and again we hear of an employer assuming the functions of a Mr. Grundy, and refusing to employ women as secretaries or typists "to avoid gossip" but their capacity for such vocations has been established times without number and is beyond dispute. The demand for larger Stock Sizes.—Boot awl glove makers have adopted themselves to the under range of sizes demanded in these days of physical exercise, but . the complaint is made that drapers and outfitters confine themselves to a too limited range of stock sizes, in ready-made garments. It is only at the principal estab- lishments that the woman 'with a 26 or 28 inch waist measuremeht may obtain what she requires, and then the range of selection is a very limited one. That is one of the misfortunes of being " too abundant." The medium in all things is the happy state, but, unfortu- nately Nature has a way of getting the best of us when the matter in dispute is our desired avoir-du-pois. No doubt the drapers know their own business best, but there are good grounds for the suggestion that they should keep larger stock sizes, especially in blouses, coats and skirts.

Furs.—The prices of furs are already rising, and we should do well to purchase what we require in this direction without delay. Fox furs—after sable of course—will reian supreme, blue and black fox leading. Silver fox needs to be chosen with care, the hest having dark, indeed almost black necks, with scarcely a white hair visible. White fox will be worn by young girls only, being a fur that seldom looks well save against the rosy cheeks of a d4butante. Pointed fox, as it is called, should be avoided, it is cheap and exceedingly nasty, and the dye comes off on one's neck. Failing fox, we should select bear, and remember that a small piece of really good fur is worth furlongs of faked rubbish. Tailor Modes.—The nation of wearing plain coats with striped or checked skirts, and plain skirts with striped or checked coats, is one that is finding much favour among first rate tailors, the colour of the plain material matching the prenailing hue seen in the check or stripe. Such costumes are particularly smart, and exclusive-looking, and could he quite safely under- taken by the clever home worker. The majority of amateurs, however, would be wise to select the plain coat and checked or stripe.d skirt, this arrangement usually .proving the best and easiest when passing through the building process.

At Leigh, on Wednesday, Arthur D. Ch-idwick was fined for assaulting his two married sisters. Com- plainants were staying overnight at their mother's house, when defendant, whilst drunk, entered their bed- room, ordered them to go home, kicked them about, and knocked down his mother.

The Examiner,

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.] THE VERDICT OF hlhill

HEART.

By CHARLES GARVICE,

Author of "Her Heart's Desire," "In Cupid'o Chains," "Just a Girl," "Nance," "The Out- ! cast of the Family," "A Coronet of Shame," Bre.

syxorsis OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: The story opens in Australia,. whore Ralph Farringdon is present at the deathbed of his mother. They are poor, and she has been deserted by her husband, Ralph's step-father. With almost her last breath, she tells Ralph of papers in her box which are of importance to him. Her words are overheard by a man with •the appearance of a tramp, who steals into the house and abstracts the contents of the box. The scene then changes to Lynne Court, ! where Lord Lynborough, aged and in failing.. health, is in residence, his beautiful niece, • Veronica Denby, ruling the household as chate- leine. One day the Earl sends for Veronica, and informs her that it is his intention to leave her by will his whole fortune, his nephew, Talbot Denby, succeeding only to the title and the entailed estates. He asks if she will accept the condition that she wed only a man of title. Veronica, whc is as proud as she is fair, and who has known grinding poverty during her father's life, readily consents. Ridinsi, afterwards on the neighbouring moor she is struck with the gallant act of a young stranger, who snatches a puppy from under her horse's feet, receiving some slight injury in conse- quence. He refuses pecuniary reward, but asks to be taken on as one on the Earl's gamekeepers, Lord' Lynborough, coming .up in his carriage, refers the applicant to his head man. The stranger, who gives the name of Ralph Farringdon, and says he has come from Australia, obtains .the coveted job. The reader is now taken to London, and Talbot Denby is introduced—a rising, young Conservative M.P., ambitious, talented, but a con- firmed gambler of the worst type. Veronica sees Ralph Farringdon fishing in the park stream, and is at once arttracted and irritated by the ease of his manner towards her, which is not that of a dependant. He shows her how to throw the line, and in her attempts she gets the hook in her wrist. Ralph cuts it out, and applies his lips to the wound to draw out any possible poison. At that moment, Talbot Denby—who has been informed of the Earl's testamentary resolve, and hence wishes to marry Veronica—comes" upon the pair. Talbot insults Ralph, who replies coolly, and meets a threat of violence by seizing Denby's stick and, breaking it. Talbot complains to the Earl, who, however, after hearing Ralph's version of the affair, takes no action. Veronica, angry with herself for her rising interest ha Ralph, treats him with stinging contempt. She meets with an accident, however, when riding, and is touched with remorse at the tender and respectful manner in which he carries her to a neighbouring hut, and attends to her injured foot. •

• CHAPTER XI. With the assistance of Goodwin, who was much 1 • more flurried and upset than her mistress, 'Veronica hobbled to her room. The doctor had : already been sent for, and he arrived by the time . she had exchanged her habit for one of the loose and costly robes which it pleases ladies to call a dressing-gown. . Lord Lynborough came up with him, and though he attempted to *conceal it, exhibited a concern and anxiety which surprised and touched . Veronica. 1 "Why were you without a groom ?" he asked, ! ; severely. "Are you in much pain? Isn't the foot very much swollen? Are you sure it's only a sprain ? " . "I sent Brown back," said Veronica, almost humbly. "There are times when it annoys me to have him pounding after me; I, enjoy my ride so much more when 1 am alone." . "I daresay. We most of us enjoy being alone and unhampered by attendants, but—" He shrugged his shoulders as he looked down at her . with his cynical smile. : "It is only a sprain, there is nothing broken, I am rejoiced to find," said the old-fashioned doctor, in his courtly way. "It was very wise of you to have the boot removed."' "It was not I who thought of it, but—Ralph Farringdon, the gamekeeper," said Veronica, reluctantly, as if she could not withhold from him his due. "It was he who caught the mare, and car—took me to the hut and—sent for the carriage, and--" Lord Lynborough frowned. "It seems to me . that this young man is very much in evidence. ' But I suppose you rewarded him? If not, I will send for him—" "Oh, no, no!" sprang from her lips, hastily; "please don't do anything of the sort. I moan" —the colour came to her face—"l mean that he would not like it." . ' "Alt, yes; I remember he refused the sovereign I offered him the other day," remarked the Earl. "A singular young man! Such pride is rather uncommon in men of his class. But I am glad he was so thoughtful, Lind I will send for him and thank him.' "I don't think you need do so," said Veronica, orith a fine attempt at casualness. "I thanked him." "Ah, welt i - assented the Earl. "But I trust this will be a lesson to you not to go out un- attended for the future." "Oh, yes, the lesson is there all right enough," said Veronica, looking down at her foot on which the doctor was skilfully putting a cold compress. "I hope I shan't be lame long, Dr. Thorne ? " "Lame! He was shocked at so serious a word in connection with the great lady of the Court. "Oh, no, my dear young lady; it depends upon yourself. Rest, rest is the most efficacious cure for a sprain. I will come again this evening and see how you are getting on. Now, mind, I beg of you; rest—perfect rest." • "That young fellow has saved Miss Denby a week in bed at least," he said to the Earl as they went downstairs. "If he had allowed the boot to remain on, or had let her use the foot, the inflammation would haVe been more serious. • Who do you say he is? I don't remember the name." "Humph!" responded the Earl. "A new keeper. A good-looking young fellow, who is a . kind of favourite with Miss Denby. You are sure there is nothing more than a sprain? You , will look in this evening, without fail ? " ; Veronica got rid of Goodwin as soon as she , could, and closing her eyes leant back and . recalled the incidents of the morning. It was not 'only her foot that was burning—she was burning all over with a sensation that was not far removed from shame and self-reproach. She had permitted Ralph Farringdon to carry her—he had held her in his arms twice that morning !—but, worse, she had—there was no use in endeavouring to stifle or dodge her accusing conscience—hobnobbed with him—there was no other word for it—as if he had been her equal; and, still worse, she had used all her feminine artifices to win his regard, to ensnare his admiration! In the moments of her physical weakness, under the strain of her pain and the influence of her gratitude, the meaning of the course she was pursuing had not been realised by her; but now, now she was away from him, she realised it fully, and it sent hot waves of blood through her veins. She confessed to herself that she had not felt affronted, indignant when he had put his arms round her; that she had been conscious of a sense of pleasure in his society ;'that the sound of his voice was like music in her ears, and never more pleasant than when he had faltered under the influence of her eyes, her smile; that she had not been anxious for the carriage to arrive, was, indeed, almost sorry, notwithstanding the pain, when it did conic; and that she had tempted. invited, almost forced him to take her in his arms again. "Oh, I must be lost to all sense of shame and decency! Proud! I can't have more pride than a beggar woman!" she almost moaned. 'To condescend to flirt—yes, I did flirt with him— with a—a—gamekeeper ! Have I gone mad suddenly, or is it some bad strain of blood in me ? How strong'he was—and yet how gentle! .ul how -ielicately considerate. It was he who was anx-ous for the carriage to come. No gentleman could have been more consiilerate, more careful. And I behaved like—like a Fanny Mason! Yes, that describes my conduct. I took \advantage of my position, of his helplessness luid chivalry, and made him admire me, and— and--- Oh, I'M not fit to live—here at Lyne Court, at any rate, unless as a housemaid. Yes, that is my proper place, for :T. have behaved jus,t like one. ' Perhaps if I'd be a housemaid he would have responded, for I invited him. I did, 1 did. Yes; I must get Lord Lynb3rough send hire away ! " Her cheeks were still burning when Goodwir came in on tiptoe. "For goodness' sake don't creep about as if 1 were dying!" remonstrated Veronica, with novel sharpness. "I beg your pardon, miss!" said poor Good- win. still flurried and emotional. "It's Fanny Masan. She came this morning." "Oh, let her come in," said Veronica. The mention, the appearance of Fanny acted like a cold douche, and brought her back to het senses. Fanny came in, all respectful concern and anxiety. "Oh, miss, it's so kind of you to me," she said, hurriedly, "and I do hope it'. nothing serious. I met Mr. Ralph just now, and he said it was a bad sprain he was aleared. How fortunate he should have been on the spot! Bid there, I told him last night that he was a lucky young man ! " "Last night ? " said Veronica, rather coldly. "Yes, miss; he looked in with the fish you sa kindly sent us, and stopped and had a bit of supper. Mother is always so pleased when he drops in, he's so pleasant and friendly and nice; so different to the other keepers and men on the estate. He seemed quite anxious about you, miss, when I met him just now; and he said as how you were so brave, and made light of the hurt, though he knew it must have pained you terrible—" "Ah, Tea, yes, I daresay," said Veronica, breaking in upon the glib and fluent words, every one of which seemed to jar upon her inex- plicably. "There's a dress I put aside for you, Fanny. Goodwin has it; it's too slight for her. I am glad "—stemming Fanny's torrent of gratitude—"l am glad 'Ralph Farringdon cornea and sees you. He must feel lonely sometimes. And it's very nice of you to be so kind to him. Good-bye. I hope the dress will not want much altering; it won't, I think, for you and I are ' about the same size." "And they call you proud, miss!" ' broke, apparently. involuntarily, from Fanny's lips. Veronica turned away, her face suddenly scarlet. "Proud!" She laughed bitterly. "Oh, that's a mistake, Fanny, isn't it? Good-bye." She lay for hours on the sofa trying not to think of Ralph, or, If she must think of him, trying to couple him in her thoughts with Fanny ,Mason • but at last she could bear the silence and solitude of the room no longer, and insisted upon being carried downstairs and on to the terrace. But, alas! it was the very worst thing she could have done if she wanted to escape from thinking of Ralph, for she had not been reclining in the deck chair many minutes before the Earl came out, leaning upon his stick, and, looking down at her considerately, said: "The young fellow has brought up your boot and wants to know how you are, Veronica." She made a peevish little movement with the shoulder nearest him. "Oh, well, tell him I'm all right," she said. "What on earth was the use of bringing the boot It is of no use." The garl was a just man, though a proud and stern. 'He did right to bring it,', he said, laconically. "I'll thank him and send him away." "Yes—but please don't offer him money," said Veronica. The Earl smiled grimly. "If you can't trust to my discretion, you'd better thank him your- self. Send Farringdon here," he said to the footman in attendance. Veronica opened her lips to stop him, but,witn a sense of helplessness, turned over on her side and lay with her cheek in her hand, fighting to keep the colour from her face. She did not raise her eyes as she heard Ralph's step, but she was obliged to do so when the Earl said: "Farringdon, Miss Denby desires to thank you for your services this morning. I understand that but for your promptitude and resource her accident might have been more serious even than it is."

CHAPTER XII.

"If yon

Ralph left the terrace in a rage with himself. Why hadn't he returned the handkerchief with the boot? Why had he been such an idiot as to tell a kind of lie? Surely nothing would have been easier than to have taken the thing from his ; pocket and given it to her; and yet he had felt— and still felt—that he could not part with it. Why on earth should she care whether she got it back or not? Surely she must have too many l•—nlkerchiefs to miss one; to care whether it wniv lost or not?

"I'm

He felt strangely disturbed and restless; the incidents of the morning pestered him; he could not forget Veronica's face or her voice, though he tried to do 80, and was full of scorn, for himself because he could not succeed.

He tramped for many a mile over the estate, walking quickly as a man will when he is trying to get away from his thoughts; and on his round, PSI he passed the Do a and Owl. he stonoed and went in tor a glass of ale and a general survey. The tramp with the bandaged hand was sitting in the bar as Ralph approached, and, as he entered, the man got up quickly and dived into the back of the premises. The house seemed

"If there is nothing

-

fromlvhich a tasty bone has been snatched—and with an awful oath flung out his hands. "You thief!" he cried, hoarsely. "You thief! Gimme back my property ! " In his fury he shook himself free from Ralph's grasp and flung himself upon him, snarling and swearing savagely. Ralph grappled with him—it was difficult to avoid handling the man roughly— and flung him to the ground. "Get up," he said, sternly. "Get up and go; but remember the warning I gave you. If I find you on the Court lands again, give you in charge for trespassing or poaching. There's your pocket-boo k. " The man snatched at it and returned it to its place. His face was livid with fury as he shook his fist in Ralph's face.

" You—you-Lhound ! You'll find that this is the worst night's work you've ever done! You've turned your best friend agen you. You've— Yah ! I'll be even with you if it costs me a fortune," he snarled, tugging at his neck which still felt the pressure of Ralph's hand. "Be off!" said Ralph. "Be off, and thank your stars that you've got clear. And remember what I say--" "Yes. And you remember what I say!". retorted the man, furiously; "for as sure as my name's Jim Oatway, I'll make you repent this night's work!" With another snarl and a muttered imprecd- Um?, the man slunk off; and Ralph, after watch- ing him for a moment, vaulted the fence and tramped homewards. "That's done me good," he said, cheerfully. "I wanted a row with someone, and Mr. James Oatway was kind enough to oblige. I wonder what was in that pocket-book ? "

"People wondered where I had disappeared to," she said, "but the fact was that from my eighteenth year I had always been delicate. I grew gradually weaker, until after my marriage I was helpless and able to do little for my husband or children. My nerves were completely shattered and dreadful headaches quite prostrated me. Doctors were consulted, but it seemed as though medicine was useless. I was wasting away rapidly through pernicious anemia, without strength to dress myself. For twelve months I hardly knew what restful sleep was. "One day my husband said he was determined that I should give Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People a trial. WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

A meeting of this Board was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr. NV. Houghton presiding. There were also present :—Mrs. Wilkinson, Mrs. Simpson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. M. Ryan, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, X. Billinge, W. Gill, J. Evans, W. C. Brooks. S. Davies, A. Brooks, G. Mather, M. W. Seton, W. Furness, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendant of Cottage Homes.).

===== 0002601_19070824_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
TREATMENT OF IMBECILES.

The Conway Guardians wrote asking the Board to- support a resolution drawing attention to the desirability of establishing homes far imbeciles with a view to• relieving the present undue pressure on the accommoda- tion of lunatic asylums and Workhouses believing that such persons could be more adequately cared for an& trained to do work which would be advantageous to the institutions as well as to the inmates.

The CLERK said the subject was at present before. the Lancashire Board of Guardians. A short time ago a meeting was held at Preston and a committee repre- senting tbe Councils and the various Unions was. appointed to take the matter in band. The CHAIRMAN: It is no new movement. There is a great deal to be said on both sides.

INMATE WRITES TO THE LOCAL GOVERNMENT BGARD. The Local Government Board wrote stating that they had replied as follows to a commnnication from an inmate named Benjamin Budge :—" Sir, 1 am directed by the Local Government Board to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 28th ult. and to state that subject to the regulations in force, the question of a Workhouse inmates' temporary leave of absence is one for the Guardians to decide. Any complaint you may desire to make should be laid in the first instance before the Visiting Committee of the Board of G uardia ns."

PENKETH FLOWER SHOW.

Fine weather attended the holding of the annual exhibition promoted by the Penketh, Sankey and District Garden and Cage Bird Society. on Saturday afternoon in a field off Warrington-road, Penketh. Last year the prize money was £25, which was an increase on the previous year, but this year it jumped to £2, and it was the means of attracting a very good entry. The gardens were judged on Wednesday, and the judges were loud in praise of the interest displayed in them by the cottagers. The Tannery allotment gardens as well as those in Hood-lane were pictures to admire. The exhibits at. the show were also of a moderately good class having due regard to the rainy season, and only fell a little short of last year's display in excell- ence of quality. But if the weather conditions are not. favourable the very hest cannot be expected, though it must be admitte.l that in some instances the exhibits did not betoken the angry mood in which the weather clerk had been for the past. few months. There were some grand specimens of peas, potatoes and vegetables, while in flowers, the dahlias and sweet peas led the way. In the cage-bird section. there was a decrease in entries, but the quality of the exhibits was no to the standard. and the judge had some little difficulty in allotting the prizes. The show was opened shortly after three o'clock by the President, Mr. :f. G. Withinshaw, in the absence of Mr. James Dodds. Mr. Withinshaw said he was sorry Mr. Dodds was unable to open the show for them owing to indisposition. They sympathized with him very much, and hoped he would soon be restored to .bealth. He was slso sorry owing to the cold and damp season they were prevented from having a much better show, though the exhibits they had reflected great credit upon those who had entered them. Another year the com- mittee hoped by arranging several improvements to have a much larger exhibition, providing, of course, that the weather was favourable. There were seseral new attractions this year, and they were deeply obliged to their Runcorn friends for coming to help them. He had much pleasure in declaring the show open. PRIZE LIST.

Motor Cars in Collision

There was an alarming accident near Northwich on Sunday, two motor cars colliding at the crossroads, San ly Brow, Little Bud worth, between Northwich and Tarporley. One belonged to Messrs. Bellian and Davies, of Warrington, and was t. very powerful Darracq, weighing about. two tone. It contained several tradesmen of the town, who escaped unhurt, and the car itself was very slightly injured. The other, a much lighter car, fared far worse. It was owned and driven by Mr. W. F. Goldstraw, jeweller, Leek, Staffordshire and ran into the back of the Warrington car. The occupants were thrown Out, and two of them, Messrs. Hammond and Grace, sustained cuts and bruises. All the members of the party were badly shaken. The car itself was practically wrecked. Colonel Walker kindly placed his car at the disposal of Mr. Goldstraw and party. They were driven to Northvoich where they received every attention. Mr. Thompson, of the firm of Messrs. W. Thompson and Sons, Castle-street, motor and cycle works, went to the scene of the accident and with considerable diffi- culty he succeeded in'removing the car from the roadway on to the premises of Colonel Walker, all the wheels being smashed.

! "To-morrow morning, maybe." She plucked 'up the moss, but said nothing. "It is folly to go on like this," said Lockwood, sternly. "You must let me help you. There is a cottage ' some half-mile from here. A trap can be sent 1 there for you."

" asked Lockwood, I with a swift look of pain on his handsome face. "Not very. I can bear it!" Whatever his manner might be, the hermit proved himself the tenderest and most capable of cavaliers. If Teresa's "twist" had been a real instead of a sham one, she could have 03vered the way to Beech Cottage without much pain. Lockwood stood a little over six feet, and, I though slight, was well knit, with muscles of ; steel; and Teresa was a light weight. He almost I carried her the greater part of the way, and I appeared to feel it no more than if the tall, I slender girl had been a baby. He seldom spoke, ; and never except to answer her, which he did briefly, as if something within him rebelled against the task which mere manhood—not to speak of gentilesse oblige—compelled him to perform. His stern silence seemed to repress Teresa; or was some new, as yet wholly incomprehensible, feeling taking possession of her warm young heart during that transit through the fields? "There is the cottage," she said at last, break- ing one of the long silences. "Your penance is near its close, Mr. Lockwood." He drew a quick, silent breath. Was it penance? Could flesh and blood that was mascu- line be indifferent to this winsome companion- ship—he the helper, too, and she the helped? She leant on his arm; so close to him that her soft brnath almost fanned his cheek; sometimes —liftiag her over some rough place—his arm was round her; he could feel the quick throb of her ,heart. Was not his very reticence a blind struggle against the forces that were drawing him on? I will say good-bye here, and I hope you will soon be well.' "Thank you—and thank you so much for your help." She lifted her liquid grey eyes to him and held out her hand. He took it in his own, and the touch went straight to his heart. He released her with an effort, and said, quietly: "No man could have done less. Good evening." He lifted his hat and turned away.

Teresa stood still, watching him till he vanished round a turn in the lane. There was a half smile on her lips, but her eyes looked wistful. She was elated—she had scored; and yet, she was not quite as pleased with her success as she ought to have been. But it was not complete success. -Lockwood had broken away from the fetters— that would not do 1 The girl heard her name called, and turned to see Maggie's amazed face at the door.

"The twist? No need, mavourn- een ; a day or two will make it well. Besides-" She stopped. "Never mind, I've another card up my sleeve 1" "Take care, Teresa!" But Teresa only laughed and limped into the house.

She stayed indoors, or only went as far as the garden for the next week. "He'll miss me! said the witch to herself, with no idea of the forces she was playing with in Wilmot Lock- wood's nature and her own.

But if he missed her he gave no sign; if he walked along the accustomed ways, with unac- knowleded hopes, he did not betray himself. He fought still, fought fiercely, against the enemy— the enemy that wore so fair a face, with soft, xeet eyes that witched his soul, and lips he would have given all that he possessed to hold beneath his own.

But the week which was to do so much towards subjugating Wilmot Lockwood had an unlooked- for effect. Teresa had forgotten to reckon with herself! That she should vanquish the hermit was merely a matter of tune and stratagem; that the hermit should vanquish her was as com- pletely outside her calculations as if he had been an octogenarian.

And ro ! she found that she missed him—that the days dragged on drearily—that her thoughts were always dwelling on the walk through the fields, when she, impostor as she was! cheated the hermit out of kis kindness. Shame and repentance seized her. What had she been doing? She had not meant to burn him--pnly to singe; but she herself was burnt—right down into her heart. Oh, she had been wicked, foolish, unwomanly! -She did not spare herself, but used the thong with all the vigour of impul- sive, passionate youth. She must go back to Ireland; she could never face Lockwood again. THE HERMIT OF OAKDENE,

By INA LEON CASSILIS,

Author of "Is She Guilty?" "For Another's Sin," "Ivan Dmitri," "The World Against Her," "Philip Euston's Play," &e.

The girl sat on the beech-tree bough, swinging her pretty feet to and fro with the most perfect sang-froid, and a saucy smile on lips that seemed formed for smiles; the man stood looking at her. The girl was the loveliest witch that even the Emerald Isle can turn out—which is saying a great deal—fair of skin, with great dark-grey, long-lashed eyes, and tumble of silky chestnut curls over her head. Could anything masculine look on such a creature with displeasure ? Yet the features of the man wore a stern, uncom- promising look that the girl's witchery seemed to have DO power to soften. He was not an old man; he looked, despite the settled gravity of his countenance, scarcely forty. He was a very handsome man, too—tall, slight, erect, with finely-Cut features expres4ve of great force of character; short, dark curly hair clustered over his head— hair thick sown with grey. A moustache covered his upper lip, but check and chin were clean shaved. The whole appearance of the man was distinguished and picturesque; but his face just now was forbidding enough. "Perhaps you are aware," he said at last, "that you are on private ground ? " Considering that the minx had, with the com- bined agility and mischievousness of a schoolboy, climbed a tall fence' to get into the ground the question might seem superfluous. She nodded hei curly head, on the back of which a red Tam-W- Shanter sat jauntily. "To. be sure," she replied, with the sweetest of Irish voices and touch of the brogue. "Is the ground yours ? " "It is; and I choose to keep it private. I must ask you to leave it." "H'm " said the witch, not one jot abashed, with never a blush on her clear cheek under the gaze of the brilliant dark eyes below. "That's easier said than done."

She looked down at the mossy floor some twelve feet beneath her, then along the big bough, at the extremity of which she was seated. The man said, coldly: "You climbed up; suppose you can climb down again." "Indeed, but I can't," returned the young lady, mendaciously. "I'll have to sit here until some gamekeeper comes along and helps me down.'

"There are no gamekeepers—no one will pass this way."

- "Then what'll Ido at all ! " said she. "Sure you'll help me down ? " The man looked at her steadily for a moment. How he could do so nmoved was a mystery. Perhaps he was not qtlite so unmoved as he appeared. But the girl met the scrutiny without wincing; and whether he believed her plea or not he could hardly leave her there on the chance of her falling in an attempt to retreat the way she had come.

He made a step nearer. here ? " he said, shortly. "I climbed the fence."

"Why did you do it?" "For a lark."

"How did you get

"You must promise never to do that again— though," correcting himself, "women don't keep promises:"

- "Yes they do," said the girl, saucily—"quite as much as men do."

"You are a child!" he answered, half scorn- fully. "Very well, let yourself go." He held out his arms and the light, slender figure dropped into them. "Thanks," she said, as he instantly released her.

She dropped a pretty curtsey and ran fleetly away; and he saw her the next moment negotia- ting the fence with all the ease of a practised gymnast. He turned back to the gloomy house among the trees. The girl ran, laughing, to a cottage some quarter of a mile distant, and sprang into the presence of a girl two or three years her senior with the enigmatical exclamation: "I've done it, Maggie 1 Such a lark 1 "

A week ago terefra-Desmond came over from Ireland on a long visit to her widowed aunt by marriage, Mrs. Venables, and her cousin Maggie, who lived very quietly—for they were not rich— in the village of Oakdene. Teresa was sunlight personified—beautiful, brilliant, brimming over with fun and love of mischief, with as yet unsounded depths in her nature. The second day after she came she asked Maggie: "Who lives in that grey house, half hidden among trees ?"_ _ _ _

"The Croft ?" said Maggie. lives there."

"The hermit

"Hermit? A real one, do you mean ?" "He is called the Hermit of Oakdene," replied the other. "His name is Lockwood. He came there two years ago, and lives quite secluded. An old woman goes in to do all that he needs,. and leaves at night. He rarely goes beyond tge grounds; but he has been seen—and he's a very handsome•fellow, and not old. No one knows anything about him. He never speaks to people. We can't make him out a bit. I believe he has committed some crime, or has somebody im- prisoned there, or something of that sort. Many of the people are half afraid of him—they think he's uncanny." "Fudge!" said Teresa. that hermit, as you call him." "I don't think even you could, Teresa. Some of the girls did try hard; but it was no use, he wouldn't look at them. I believe he is a woman- hater."

"I mean to conquer

"Fudge ! " said Teresa again, and danced away laughing. The visit to the beech-tree was the result of her determination. "You see," she said, relating the adventure to the wondering Maggie, "I could have climbed along the bough like a Monkey, or swung myself to the ground as easily as possible. I have practised all these tricks; but 1 didn't mean to do either; I meant that stern, forbidding hermit to lift me down—and he had to ! "

"How cheeky you are, Teresa! But now you've' done so much, how are you going on ? " "Oh, you wait! I don't mean to be conquered, I can tell you." "But, Teresa, suppose the man is a criminal? —gentlemen have committed crimes before now." "He hasn't, I'm sure," replied Teresa, promptly. "He is a ridiculous misanthrope; and that sort of thing ought not to be encouraged." Maggie laughed heartily. "I tell you what," she said. "I wish I had some of his grapes."

"Has he any ? " _ _ "Ye—es. The loveliest vinery, and he never gives any away; the grapes rot on the boughs." "Disgustingly selfish! " said Teresa, dog- matically. "I see that this hermit wants a thorough routing out—and he shall have it ! " It was early summer, the weather was glorious, and Teresa, sometimes with Maggie, sometimes alone, was here, there, and everywhere—in the woods, the lanes, the fields, accompanied, when otherwise alone, by a big collie dog which belonged to a neighbour, and was always delighted to have. a scamper with "the wild Irish girl." _ _

In these wanderings Teresa- several times encountered Wilmot Lockwood, the hermit of the Croft; perhaps, after the first and second times, not, on her part, altogether accidentally, for she discovered that h 6 did sometimes quit the grounds of the Croft p.nd go into the country; but it was in the most solitary part, over the moorland, and into the forest land beyond, where there was least likelihood of encountering any human being. On these occasions no salutation or overt sign of recognition passed between the Irish girl and the hermit. He strode on, hardly seeming to look at her; and she—with the intuitive wisdom of her sex—was careful not to let him suppose, by any look of hers, that there was design in the encounter. That, she said to herself, would spoil the whole game. One day, as Lockwood was walking at his visual quick pace along one of the most secluded lanes in the district, he saw, by the wayside, figure seated on the grass, with head bent down in clasped hands; a large collie dog sat dis- consolately beside the figure, which, g second glance assured him, was that of the pretty trespasser—Teresa Desmond, as his old servant had gratituously informed him. The damsel was perfectly aware of his approach; she had seen him (behind the shelter of friendly bushes) some listance off, and the tableau he beheld ,was Jesigned for his special edification. As he in- voluntarily paused, Laird, the collie, ran up to him with an imploring whine; he, poor honest beast! could not know that his mistress was a young impostor. At the same moment Teresa Lifted her head. and sbewed the loveliest tear- stained face on which the sun ever shone. Smiles and dimples and saucy challenge a man 4141/ steel himself to resist; but what man—or

===== 0002601_19070824_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
served nothing better than the unhappiness which lay before her. "Next letter father writes," she said to herself one morning, "I shall say he wants me at home. I can't stay here any more. I don't suppose lit cares ("he' was not her father!) I'm glad I failed. It was mean, contemptible, to try and conquer a man—though I never meant that he should really care ! " But Mr. Desmond did not write very often, and so Teresa had to wait for his letter. More- over she could not be too precipitate, to say nothing of a disinclination—when it came to the point, to put the sea between herself and the hermit. She should not see him—but to be in the same place was something. The girl was too proud; however, to wear her heart upon her sleeve, and she danced and sang in the old way, and seemed always in brilliant spirits. "Well, Teresa," Maggie said one afternoon, "Mr. Lockwood is obdurate. You'll have to play that other card. What is it?"

"Never mind. I'm not sure I shall play it now. Come out for a walk—it's a glorious day." "I promised to call on old Miss Jenkins," said Maggie. making a wry face. "Oh, 1 shan't go in to see her ! " said Teresa. "The once you inveigled me into her house was quite enough for me. I'll go with you as far as the wood, and wait for you; and don't be longer than you can help." "Be sure 1 wOn't; but she's such a chatterbox, and you can't be rude to an old lady." Teresa tossed on her Tam-o'-Shanter ; Maggie put on a more festive hat, and the two girls set out, the younger with a heavy heart, though the talked as brightly as ever. "What a lark," said Maggie, as they plunged into the wood, "if we porno across the hermit." "We are not likely to this way," returned the other, carelessly. "It was the moorland way he used to go." They gained the stile which parted the wood from a little bit of broken common, which Maggie had to cross, and then go down a lane to reach Miss Jenkins's house. At the stile Teresa paused. "I'll wait for you here," said she. "Aft revoir."

Maggie crossed the stile, and Teresa perched herself on the top rail. But once alone the light went out of her eyes, all the brightness vanished from her face. There was no need to wear a mask in this loneliness, and the girl had to struggle hard to keep the tears from falling fast and free. She got down from the top rail, and sat on the step, leaning her head against the rail and feeling utterly miserable and self- reproachful. A quick step quite close made her start and look up. Someone was approaching by the wood path. A second more, and she caught her breath, and her heart gave a great leap and stood still. It was Wilmot Lockwood! She rose to her feet, but dared not look at him, or she might have been still more startled. He came up to her and paused. "Miss Desmond, he said—his voice and manner seemed constrained—"l hope you are— quite well again ? " Teresa coloured scarlet, and turned aside. "Yea —thanks ! " she said, with difficulty. "Quite well." She had not offered her hand, and appeared embarrassed. Lockwood looked pained and perplexed.

"]be i your pardon," be said after a pause, which Teresa did not offer to break, but still stood with averted face. "I am afraid I have offended you in some way." Teresa shook her head. "No," she said, in a low voice. "You were very kind." "I didn't know it. I—l have been hoping to see you all these days." His voice trembled. A rush of bewilderment, of anguish, held Teresa breathless. What had she done? Conquered after all—to be despised and condemned—played with a man's heart, and only won it to lose it! She was but twenty, and her self-command broke down utterly under the strain. Her head went down on the rail, and a choking sob broke from her. Lock-- wood laid his hand on her shoulder and bent over her.

"Child," he said, hoarsely, "don't turn from me—have pity on me! lam .mad to love you, but you are stronger than I. I have struggled, but it's no use—you have conquered. 1 love you!" "Oh, don't say it—don't, don't!" cried the girl, now weeping bitterly—the more bitterly for the passionate joy in her heart. "I never thought you would care. I'm not worth your love. Oh, how can I tell you!" "Teresa "—the man's lips were white, his tones suppressed in the tense effort for self-control— "don't play with me! A woman wrecked my life years ago—made me what I am. I loved her, as I never dreamt I could love again; trusted her, and she betrayed me. I said I would never trust a woman again, and then—then—you came and when I saw your face,all that 1 thought dead leaped up within me. Teresa, speak to me! Am I—good Heavens !—too late ? " "Oh, no—no—it isn't that! Let me tell you. You'll despise me—as I deserve—when you know—" She stopped a moment; then went on, brokenly, her head still bent down the colour coming and going in her cheek: "When I heard —about you—l thought it would be fun to—to conquer you. That's why I went into your groutids that day, and it—it was all pretence about my sprain—l wasn't hurt a bit. I saw yQU coming, but indeed, indeed I didn't think you would really care—not--not love me—l never thought of that!" "No," he said, with strange quietness as she paused, his hand clasped over the rail near him with so tense a grasp that the blue veins stood out. "You didn't—think. I might have known! Is that all?"

For Teresa had stopped dead. She shook her head now. "No," she said, almost in a whisper. "When I—stayed in so long—l thought you— you would miss me—and I-- No! You won't believe me—why should you? I'm going back home-"

"Not yet, Teresa—not till I have heard all," said Lockwood, sternly. "Go on." The blood rushed over the girl's cheek and brow. "I mean;" she said, with difficulty, "that it came back on me. I found that I—that I— missed you." The curly head went right down again upon the rail; the hot colour dyed even the soft throat. But her hands were taken in a strong, warm clasp; she was drawn back within the man's arms, folded to his heart. "Teresa," he said, passionately, "you are speak- ing truth—you are not playing with me now You missed me ? You love me, Teresa? Look into my eyes and answer me ! " The girl gave one swift upward look, then hid her face on her lover's breast; but that glance was enough; it told him all he needed to know, and he bent his head and his lips took their fill of ssweetness from hers.

"You look younger already," Teresa said, softly, some twenty minntes later; they were sitting on a fallen tree now, the girl leaning on her lover's heart.

"That is your doing, dsarest," he answered, smiling down on the winiome face. "I have taken a new lease of life."

"And you have quite, quite forgiven me ? " "Sweetheart, it was but a girlish frolic; and, after all, I was conqueror as much as van- quished." "So you were ! " She hid her face again, then suddenly started up with an exclamation of Mingled dismay and amusement. For Maggie was standng on the other side of the stile, looking as if she were not sure whether what she beheld was a vision which, on closer inspection, N\vould vanish, or whether it was Teresa in the flesh whom she saw in the arms of the hermit. But the hermit himself soon explained matters, and Maggie considerately went on ahead to prepare Mrs. Venables for the actual appearance in Beech Cottage of the hermit of the Croft—not only in visible and substantial presence, but as a lover. The village has not recovered—perhaps never will recover—from the shock of the hermit's marriage, though he married a creature lovely and winsome enough to have bewitched a genuine hermit. But Wilmot Lockwood has never had cause to regret the renewal of love and faith. He worships his young wife, and she gives him back love for love in full measure • nor is there in his heart any shadow of distrust, as Teresa at first feared there might be; for it might well seem as if his faith in womanhood had been almost uprooted. But he smiled when one day she questioned, half- timidly. "Let no such thought torment you," he said, drawing her to his heart. "I could not love you as I do and for one instant doubt you. No ghost from out the past stands between you and me, Teresa. You have restored to me all that I lost—love and faith. Are you_ answered ? - _ - -

............_ . And her pure soft eyes looked up to his and said: "Yes."

THE END.]

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

Brooms that are dipped into scalding soap- suds once a week will have their bristles so toughened that they will wear much longer than brushes that are not so treated.

Stewing is the most economical mode of cooking, as *it needs little heat, and the vegetables so necessary for the flavour in- crease the bulk of the stew.

Rhubarb Fool.—Stew some rhubarb with a little sugar and lemon peel. When cold pass it through a sieve, add a gill of cream, and if that is not available two tablespoonfuls of condensed milk. Serve in a glass dish.

I splendid cream, to be applied to the hands at night to whiten the skin, is made of lanoline, one ounce; liquid paraffin, two tea- spoonfuls; oil of bergamot, five drops. Melt the lanoline and stir in the other ingredi- entS.

To keep healthy, eat good, nourishing food regularly three times a day, and between inears drink a glass of cream or milk. Drink plenty of good, pure water. Take all the rest possible, if possible an hour's sleep each afternoon. Be in the open air all you can without exercising too much.

Celery can be kept fresh a long while if it is wrapped _in thick paper, sprinkled with water, then put in a damp cloth, and stored in a cool, dark plac,e. Before preparing for table, plunge the celery into cold water, and stand for one hour.

Savoury Cutlets.—Mix a teacupful of fine breadcrumbs, two teaspoonfuls of chopped parsley, one teaspoonful of chopped onion, and half a teaspoonful of mixed herbs to- gether; season with pepper and salt. Roll the cutlets in beaten eog, then put in the above mixture as quickly as possible. Put by for one hour. Fry in quite boiling fat.

When washing painted walls or wood, put one pennyworth of painters' size (previously dissolved in hot water) in a pail of water, and apply with g soft cloth. • The dirt will be very. quickly removed and the paint im- proved' in appearance. Wet the bottom part of the paint before commencing at the top. This prevents the water leaving dirty marks on the lower part.

Marks on a polished table caused by hot plates are a special source of annoyance to the housewife. French polishing is usually considered the only remedy, but an applica- tion of paraffin well rubbed in and polished with a soft cloth produces good results. The treatment must be repeated , for several days until the spots disappear.

Mock Goose Pie.—This is a savoury way of loing up cold mutton, or, indeed, any scraps. of meat. Mince half a pound of meat, season it with boiled onions, chopped finely, quarter of a teaspoonful of powdered sage, pepper, and salt. Moisten all with milk or gravy. Place in a deep pie dish, with breadcrumbs on the top. Scatter little bits of butter over, and bake a nice brown. Serve hot.

To wash woollen stockings so that they will not shrink is quite easy. First shred some yellow soap into a small tin saucepan. Cover it with cold water and let all boil slowly on the stove till a jelly. Take some tepid water, and with the boiled soap make a good lather. Wash the stockings in this, rubbing well and using no other soap. Rinse in tepid clear water, wring out, and set in the air to dry quickly.

To cure . cramp in the legs at night, . make a garter of wide tape and on to it sew thin slices of cork cut from a wine bottle cork. Wear this regularly' every night in bed. As cramp probably comes from acidity reduce this by drinking hot water every night before going to bed. Dose, from half a pint to a pint.

Delicious Buns.—Take the weight of two eggs in flour and sugar, half their weight in butter, a teaspoonful of baking powder, and. a teaspoonful of corn flour. Mix the flour, baking powder, and corn flour well together, then add the butter, and rub it well in. Beat the eggs and sugar together, and add to the dry ingredients. Well butter some small tins and bake in a quick oven.

An excellent idea for pantry shelves is to give them two coats of ordinary white paint and then a third finishing coat of white enamel. As soon as the enamel dries, wash it over with cold water, and then it will harden quickly. Do not cover these shelves with paper, but leave them bare, and notice the improvement. The enamel is easily wiped clean with a damp cloth.

When a savoury stew is made and there are bits of bread to dispose of, let the hodse- wife try the rich effects of sippets in he-r stew. First sfie should take bits of stale bread and cut them in pieces. These should be fried in clarified dripping and slipped into the etew. They mingle With .the gravy, and are a great improvement to it.

The following is a good method to preserve butter: Pack the butter solidly in stone jars, put a clean cloth, wrung out of clear, cold water, on the top of the butter, then spread over the cloth one-third of an inch of salt. and pour on the salt one inch of water. The water and salt will form a' strong brine, mak- ing it impossible for the air to (Yet into the butter. Tie a clean cloth over the jar. Use the best dairy salt for salting the butter. Good butter, carefully packed,. and kept in a cool, dry cellar, will 'keep perfectly sweet for a long time,

To keep palms in the nouse in a healthy condition you should sponge the leaves once a week with tepid water in which is a little milk. Then stand the pot quite covered in lukewarm water for two hours. Palms treated in this manner will live a long time.

Do you find your gas stove a real econo- miser? If not, these may be " sale Of the reasons: Lighting burners before you are ready to use them. Baking but tine thing at a time. Heating water or cooking in un- covered vessels. Placing small vessels upon large burhers. Using large burners when small ones would do. Not turning out the flame before removing food. Not lowering the flames when food is already boiling.

Haricot Beans.—Steep one pound of hari- cot beans bi cold water for twenty - four hours. Put them in a pan with two quarts of cold water, and simmer gently for nearly two. hours. Drain off the water, have ready a frying-pan with two ounces of hot drip- ping, two onions sliced, and a little salt. Turn the beans into the frying pan, fry lightly, and serve with pepper and salt.

The best way to clean white paint is to rash it in warm soft water with a very little am- monia in it. Any marks that are very obsti- slate may be removed by rubbing with a little finely-powdered pumice stone. Soap should never be used to white paint, as it causes it to turn yellow.

AN EPIDEMIC OF COLDS.

The chilly nights that follow sunny days at this season of the year account for much in the prevailing epidemic of bad colds and chills. The grandest insurance against these colds and chills, which threaten serious throat and lung trouble if neglected, is a handy box of Peps. It is not necessary to take Peps constantly and continuously like cheap and useless lozenges and medicated sweetmeats. One little Pep, placed on the tongue when going out in the chilly night air, will fill the lungs with powerfully healing, and germicidal pine-fumes, which impregnate all the tissues of the throat and lungs. Peps, unlike ordinary lozenc,bes, will save pounds in doctor's bills if used upon the first sign of sore throat or chill.

Lord Ibrox may be

THE pardoned for the surprise AGRICULTURAL which he expressed at the LABOURERS attitude of the House of CHARTER. Lords on the second reading of the English Small Holdings Bill. Tne experiences of the Govern- ment in their proposed land legislation this session have not been very happy so far as the House of Lords has been concerned. The Evicted Tenants Bill which constitutes their Irish land legislation has been mercilessly criticised and altered in the Upper Chamber. The Scottish Small Landholders Bill was treated to a more I undignified snub by being put back in the teeth of Government protest until such time as the Euglish 'Bill had been considered. The probabilities, therefore, not unnaturally appeared to the Government to be that their English Bill would be received no less unfavour- ably than had been the case with their proposals regarding the Celtic fringe. Led by Lord ONSLOW, however, the Unionist Peers gave to the measure what was practically a friendly reception; and Lord CARRINGTON, who moved the second reading, pointed out that the Small Holdings Act introduced by the Unionist Party had not proved a remarkable success. But he gave them the full credit for having started upon a course which he and his colleagues found it possible to develop and extend. When the present Government came into office the conditions of land tenure and the opportunity for the small cultivator and the agricultural labourer were limited by the land system in force. Last year the Government took in hand and eventually passed the Land Tenure Bill introduced by Mr. AGAR-ROBA.RTES. That measure Lord CARRINGTON referred to with some pride as the "Farmer's Charter." The present measure is in a sense the natural corollary of last year's legislation. Its proposals are fairly simple. Its main principle is the letting of land on lease at a fair rent with security of tenure. The Government has discarded the proposal advocated so strongly by Mr. JESSE COLLINGS and other students of the land problem to make peasant ownership the basis of the scheme. That decision has been criticised, and the effect of the plan preferred by the Government will no doubt, he very closely watched in the country districts. Lord CARRINGTON declared his conviction that what people really wanted was not so much to be owners of tiny pieces of land as to have security of the laud on which they earn their living. That, of course, is a matter of opinion ; but, at any rate, the present measure will give the opportunity of testing whether hiring will prove as great an attraction to the agricultural labourer as purchase might have done. The machinery by which the Government will put its proposals into operation is simple enough. County Councils will be the authority for small holdings; Parish Councils the authority for allot- ments. The only point of real disagreement has been the question of whether the Board of Agriculture shall, through the medium of a special commission which will be set up, be able to coerce the local authorities should they seem reluctant to apply the Act. Lord BELPER, a Unionist Peer, agreed with the Government that if the commissioners acted reasonably and promptly this provision might prove satisfactory, but he warned them of the effect of holding a pistol at the head of the local authorities. So far, there is practically no serious disagreement regarding the most important piece of legislation which the Government has accom- plished during the long and trying session now coming to a close ; but it is still a matter of doubt how far this Act or any other goes towards accom- plishing the objects which social reformers of the type of Mr. RIDER HAGGARD and others have had in view. Lord ONSLOW frankly declared that in his view legislation of this kind will not , turn back the tide of migration from the country to the towns. " Back to the land "is an excellent cry, but it must be accompanied by very material advantages before it will seduce the town-dweller to leave the crowded community to go into the uupeopled countryside, and under take all the arduous and often unsatisfactory labours from which he or his immediate progenitors freed themselves when England turned from an agricultural into an industrial country. The call of the town is too strong, probably, even to permit the advantages which the agricultural labourers will receive under this measure to stem the tide of migration. It is deplorable, no doubt. The countryside is yearly becoming less like the England which our grandfathers knew. This measure will help these who are determined to find their livelihood on the land to rest secure in the knowledge that they are not at the caprice of an insecure tenure. Beyond that few people will be inc:ined to think that the benefits will extend.

Marriage with a deceased THE DICCEASED wife's sister will soon be as WIFE'S SISTER, legal as it is natural. Since Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman took this much abused measure out of private hands it has made rapid progress, and one may now regard it at one of the legislative achieve- ments of the session. The bitterness and bigotry which have been exhibited by the clerical opponents of this Bill are remarkable. Why should any reasonable person object to a marriage between any man and his deceased wife's sister ? It is difficult to imagine any sort of satis- factory reply. We always come back to the same query, " Why, after all ?" The Bishops in the House of Lords give us no answer. Nor did that keen, strange, bigoted man Lord Robert Cecil offer any answer in the House of Lords. He merely gave further illustrations of that most strange of conjunctions, a broad intellect and extreme narrowness of view. The opposition of the Bishops to the measure was, on tnis occasion, based mainly on their assumption that there has been no popular demand for this legis- lation. This is merely a demonstration of what is, perhaps, the inevitable ignorance of B.shops concerning the state of public feeling. Looking back, we are astounded at the patience with which the autocratic dominion of the narrowest-minded men in the Church of England has been borne. The National Church, as it is sometimes called, is merely a sect. It commands the real allegiance of perhaps one-fifth of the English people ; but even among this one-fifth there was difference of opinion as to the law which is now to be abrogated. Marriage with a deceased wife's sister,it is contended by the Bishops, is against the Canon Law ; but what have we to do with the Canon Law ? It is of no effect whatever unless it is supported by statutes as it has most unreasonably been in this instance. That support is now to be withdrawn. We are now, as the Lord Chancellor said, to be governed by the law of the land, and to the Bishops we offer the suggestion that though by falling in with public opinion on this subject they cannot undo all the harm they have done to the Church by their long opposition to marriages of a perfectly natural and beneficial character, they may possibly undo some of it.

The first session of the THE TRANSVAAL Transvaal Parliament bus PARLIAMENT. now come to a close, and

accounts which are to -hand from the Colony show that it has been in a large sense successful. New brooms proverbially sweep clean, and it was to be expected that the Govern- ment with General Botha at its head, which came into power after the General Election, would not be less thorough than youthful enthusiasm usually shows itself. The Parliament has estab- lished a fairly good record. It has passed more than forty bills, or nearly a bill a day. On both the Dutch and the English sides of the House good reputations have been made by individual Members, and there have been few indications of anything other that sincere desire for the benefit of the Colony and of South Africa. Perhaps it is the admirable spirit shown by the Government and by the Chamber which is the most gratifying fact as far as English people are concerned. The grant of Leif-government was a bold experiment, but the first session of the Legislature has proved that it was not unjustified. The final act, indeed, of the session, the decision to present the Cullman diamond to the King, has demonstratei that among the Dutch the spirit of loyalty has already become implanted. The Colony has no doubt many trials to go through in the future; but the good start which has been made is a happy augury.

Mr. Roosevelt has made it STILL FIGHTING quite clear in his latest THE TRUSTS, speech that he and his Government do not intend to abandon their fight against the Trusts. He has a great principle at stake, which he stated succinctly enough in his opening remarks. "There is a growing determination," he said, "that no man shall amass a great fortune by special privilege, chicanery, and wrongdoing, as far as it is in the power of legislation to prevent it, and that fortune when amassed shall not have a business use that is anti-social," Unfortunately, the law has never yet in any country been able to catch up with dis honesty. And Mr. Roosevelt's legislation will stop short of anything like prevention of what is known as " graft," though it may have the effect of checking the more glaringly unrighteous opera- tions of the Trusts. The difficulties which stand in the way, however, are becoming daily more apparent. Most things in America are a matter of dollars. Just now the markets are having a bad time ; depression and panic have unsettled the stability of Wall-street. So the President, with all his brave words, was under the necessity of saying things which would have a less alarm- ing effect than his hitherto outspoken declarations. He went no further, it is true, than to say that corporations which are operated within the law, and not hurtful to the common good, will not be interfered with. That, however, is an elastic phrase, of which American politicians and business men are able to gauge the precise value ; and the fact that Wall-street has been comforted is a somewhat ominous sign. In this country sympathy has been, and will continue to be, with Mr. Roosevelt. The Trust system has evils which are recognised and- feared, and a blow at it, even though it is outside our own immediate circle of interest, is welcomed with enthusiasm.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

It is surprising what strange and indefensible expressions creep into our language. At Tuesday's meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians a member in referring to the conduct of a man mentioned in connection with a relief case said he had seen him "as drunk as a boiled owl." Another Guardian asked for this stage of drunkenness to be defined, as he himself had never seen a boiled owl! No answer was forthcoming. o

"Recently I had an oiler of marriage made to me over the telephone from a man who had never seen me and who had only seen my photograph for the first time, a few hours before he rang me up. He said that he always acted on impulse, had five thousand a year, and was considered good-looking. I replied, always act on impulse, too. No thanks' good- bye,' and rang olT."—Miss BILLIE BURKE in " London Opinion."

It is not necessary to go abroad for examples of "English as she is spoke.' Our charwoman (writes a "Manchester Guardian " correspondent) was angrily describing her son's misdemeanours. "'E's the two-facest lad I ever see," was her bitter summary of his qualities. Another neat and original character sketch VMS uttered by a man who came to refit our telephone wires, of which the arrangement had been disapproved by an inspector. We told him what sort of man the inspector was, and the fitter seemed to recognize the portrait. "Ali know 'im," he said; "it'll be Jones—Bill Jones. There's two on 'em, Bill and 'Enery, an' they're both two swines."

* Most people are aware t*ha*t the Chiltern Hundreds, of which Mr. Blake, the member for South Longford, has just been appointed steward and bailiff, are one way out of the House of Commons. But perhaps the original nature of that mysterious office is less generally known. The Chiltern Hundreds are a tract of land. on the side of the Chiltern Hills, in Buckinghamshire, consisting of the Hundreds of Bodenham, Desborough, and Stoke. To prevent the incursions of robbers from the cover of the beech forests on the hills a steward was appointed by the Crown. That steward- ship is now a sinecure, and exists only to enable members of Parliament to "take office under the Crown," and so vacate their seats.

Congratulations to our local custodians of the peace ,on the success of their ninth annual sports. They were quite favoured in being allowed to carry the programme through almost entirely without rain. •Success was never better deserved for the "men in blue" always throw themselves with great zeal into this annual effort on behalf of charitable institutions. The meeting certainly does them credit and worthily holds a Popular place among local athletic -events. * * *

The Clerk said the cases were" not on all fours." The cyclist might have retorted that the horse was at any rate. TEMPLE THURSTON: i

Judging by the experiences of last week, we cannot ,compliment the Warrington theatre-goers on their manners. During the most tender passages of '" Romeo and Juliet" there were cat-calls from all parts of the house and smacking mock kisses at every opportunity. This behaviour must not only have been very trying for the artistes on the stage, but it was excessively annoying to those members of the audience who had a modicum of decency and respect in their composition. The strangest thing was that this hoggish behaviour was not confined to "the gods" or poorer parts of the house by any means. People who indulge in this sort of thing should have the fire hose turned on them. It would cool their osculatory ardour. * *

In these utilitarian days It is pleasing to note what Iteadway the culture of flowers M making in the district. The shows held in the villages around Warrington have shown whit a pride many people take in their gardens, and too much cannot be said in praise of the societies which encourage such a healthful and elevating pursuit. Progress has been reported all round, and at Hatton on Thursday the entries -totalled the record number of 670. We wish every success to those who are doing so much to preserve the beauties of the country. Neither is the town being over-looked and the show to be hold by the 'Beautiful Warrington Society will demonstrate that choice blooms can be grown even in smoky Warring- ton.

* * * The Chicago School Board has just chiselled out the name of Henry George from the lintel of one of their public schools, and substituted the name of George M. Pullman, of Pullman car fame! In a comment,the "Chicago Examiner" says : —" Henry George was not sufficiently commercial and was therefore a dangerous ideal to hold up to American youth. He was merely a thinker, a student of social conditions, a friend of the people. He seemed to be inoculated with the pernicious idea that taxation should be just. 'Those who deny his conclusions will not deny that this was his basic idea. Such a suggestion in any form could not fail to wound deeply the sensibilities of Mr. Busse's aggregation of educational experts. But Mr. George M. Pullman—there was their ideal. Ile was a money-grubber. He was a tax-dodger after their own hearts. He amassed millions. His name will be remembered as long as the sleeping car takes its toll from the public. He stood for the dollar. -The other man's concern was humanity." * * An innocent correspondent complains of the shock , be receives from the newspaper bills announcing the I progress of the peaceful game of cricket. What I complain of (he writes) is that a half-glance does not reveal the fact that cricket is referred to. , All that strikes my eye is a word or tiro of vaguely tremendous import—" sensational," "terrific," and so on—and my heart throbs with the idea of some great world- catastrophe. lam becoming callous to "sensational collapse" (cricket must be a very exhausting game, for this accident seems very common). But I protest against the signs of further enterprise in this direc- tion; very soon the whole vocabulary of thrill will have been appropriated to the game. I expect to see:

"The line must be drawn somewhere. ft * Our good friend "The Dawn" appears to be inflicted with a serial story as protracted as an illness. Even the hero, we read this month, is suffering from an " overpowering sense of drowsiness." The story relates the following wonderful circumstance : " For months past, all his friends had noticed how he would sink into a deep and heavy slumber almost as soon as he had laid his head upon his pillow, sometimes in the very middle of a sentence." It is certainly unusual for all one's friends, or even R few, to be present at the moment when one's head toueheq the pillow, even if, more remarkable still, it happens to be in "the vary middle of a sentence." 'Harold Wolfgang seems to have got the "Bleeping sickness" badly, and is at the same time endangering both his friends and his readers. He has already fallen asleep in the pulpit, in the study, in the bath- room, and in the presence of his sweetheart. Even vigorous prods won't wake him. We are getting interested in ilarold, who bids fair to rival " Phra Phoenician"the or "Rip Van Winkle." There is just a chance his prospective marriage may wake him up !

Conscientious Objection. At the County Petty Sessions held at Warrington, on Wednesday, Thomas R Roylance. applied for an exemption order on the grounds that vaccination would be prejudicial to the child's health. He said that he himself suffered seriously through vaccination. —When asked whether he wcul-d rather run the risk of the child having small-pox, he replied that he did not think that vaccination had anything to do with small-pox at all.—The order was granted.

The Late Colonel Ridgway. Before commencing the ordinary business at the C-Junty Petty Sessions at Warrington on Wednesday, the Chairman (Capt. Withington) said he had a few words to say in reference to the late Colonel J. C. Ridgway. He remembered the time when the late Colonel was sitting on his right hand side. He sympathised with the deceased's relatives and friends. Mr. Henry Greenall (magistrates clerk), said the late Colonel was always willing and assiduous in his duties. Mr. Browne on behalf of the Bar, also spoke in sympathetic terms in reference to the late Colonel. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Music In the Park.
