Negligent Husband. • At the Warrington Borough Court on Wednesday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, Annie Gill, I' Stoney-lane, summoned her husband George Gill, lsst from Sale, for maintenance arrears to the amount of £3 Os. 6d. due under a separation order granted in July. —The Chief Constable said the husband was a very clever workman and could earn good wages. He spent too much of his money in drink. —Prisoner now said he would behave differently. —The Mayor said if he could find a sovereign before two o'clock the case would be adjourned for a month but if not he would have to go to gaol for a month. Death of Mr. C. H. Bickford. Many of our readers will deeply regret to hear of the death of Mr. Charles Henry Bickford, which took place at his residence, Grappenhall-road, Stockton Heath, on Thursday of last week. The deceased gentleman was closely identified with the commercial life of Warrington having been connected with the Great Northern Railway for about 30 years. Until three years ago he held the position of goods manager at Warrington for the Lancashire and Cheshire district, his retirement being occasioned by ill-health. He was of a kindly and genial disposition and was deeply respected. The funeral took place at St. Thomas's Church,on Monday where there was a large attendance of relatives and frieilds.

P.S.A. Meetings. A well-attended meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society was held on Sunday when the Rev. J. Thomas, of Lancashire College, presided, and spoke upon "The Fatherhood of God and " The Brotherhood of man." Mr. Barbery, of Oldham, gave the address upon" The Compassion of Jesus." He said the multitudes looked up to God and acknowledged His power and greatness but they did not live to Him and work for Him. God wanted the people, and had given his great love for them, and desired their Salvation. Mr. W. lives took part in the meeting. Mr. A. Griffiths sang" The Good Shepherd," which was well, received. At the close of the meeting a friend in the meeting, who is a stranger, conveyed to the brethren the greetings of the Bible class and P.S.A. of Northampton. Mr. Farrar thanked the friend for his kind message, and returned the greeting to the brothren at Northampton.—The weekly meeting of the Warrington Society was held on Sunday, the Vice-President (Mr. A. Freeman) occupied the chair, awl gave a short address on "Is there a God ?" Mr. C. Unsworth played two violin solos entitled " The Holy City" and " Ora pro nobis" in a pleasing manner. Mr. H..Tonge was the accom- panist. Mr. T. L. Robins gave a yery interesting address. The President (Mr. R. Nelson) and Mr H. Warham also took part in the meeting. .

TOASTING CHEESE, -4d.,5d.,6d. per lb. ASHLEY.S.—Advt.

Theft of Provender.—Smart Capture. At the Borough Police Court yesterday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, John Ravenscroft, 20, Greenall-street, was charged with 'stealing a quantity of provender and bran to the value of Ms. 7d, the property of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. The Chief Constable said the prisoner had been employed by the company for 33 years. Shortly after midnight P.C. Hughes was on duty in Knutsford- road near the Brook Inn when he heard a noise and on going to ascertain the cause he saw prisoner who said he was there in connection with his work. The officer's suspicions were aroused and he searched the neighbourhood. He found the stolen property in a field and awaited developments. In a short time he saw the prisoner take two of the sacks away and ultimately return for the other. The officer then questioned him and he admitted taking• the provender. He said he had carried two sacks to the stable of a Mr. Walker. The constable went there and found the sacks. Prisoner bore a good character.—Ravenscroft informed the Bench that he was "hard up "and wanted to buy some shoes. He had to do a great deal of work for very little money.— Mr. Bridgewater, manager for the Canal Company, said prisoner received a guinea a week and a monthly bonus, and as he had only one arm it was almost a charity to employ him. The offence was aggravated by the fact that the animals were robbed of their food. The Bench ordered prisoner to pay 205., or go to gaol for 14 days and they complimented P.C. Hughes on his smart capture.

LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK. BRIDGE-STREET.—Adtv.

Ancient Order of Foresters.

Past District Chief Ranger Peter Peacock, sub- mitted his report of Court 430 Warrington, on Satur- day evening last, at the Blue Lion Inn, as delegate to the High Court of the Order, held at Leeds, August Bank Holiday week. There was a good attendance of members and officers.--Bro. Peacock gave an interest- ing account of the week's work. It. was gratifying to find during the year ending April 30th, there had been an increase in membership of 1,372, and in the funds of £303,198 ; there being 921,085 members with a total fund of £8,635,632. There were a lot of amendments to General Laws, resulting in a pledge of 871 delegates to the principle of compulsory olrl age pensions being , provided for new members, details of which are beilig prepared for submitting to the next High Court, which will be held at Northampton. Daring the week grants amounting to £1,339 were made to various Courts, and the ouggested centralisation with a Board of Directors for the Management of the Order was defeated. Great importance was attached to the question of amalgamation of Courts, who by reason of their numerical strength or insolvency, and this would particularly apply to one or two Courts in the Warrington District to-day, with a view of reducing the management expenses and concentrating their efforts. An important law was proposed to enable new member entrants to pay an increased fee, and immedi- ately become entitled to full benefits; Warrington members would appreciate this as being the means of defeating tontine or dividing clubs, and it is with regret that important proposal was defeated. Bro. Peacock explained the entbrtainments and outings which were arranged by the Leeds "xecutive Com- mittee, including excursions to Harrogate and Scarborough, together with a garden party at Lord Airedale's hall, (better known as Sir James Kitson), and were very much appreciated by the delegates. The members. of Court 430 passed a vote of thanks to Bro. Peacock for his interesting report, and same was suitably acknowledged.

WARRINGTON PO LICE SPORTS. SUCCESSFUL MEETING.

The Warrington police have long enjoyed a reputa- tion for the willingness of their efforts on behalf of charitable causes, and the fact that the proceeds of their annual sports are devoted to deserving local institutions always ensures generous support on the part of the townspeople. Of course, this is not the only reason why the sports are so successful. The admirable programme is in itself a great attraction, and no doubt would compare favour- ably with the events at any mid-week meet- ing in the country. Thursday witnessed the the ninth festival of the series and a crowd of between 3,000 and 4,000 assembled on the Wilderspool Football Ground. The weather was not at all in keeping with August, for there were heavy skies and a chill breeze. A. cause for thankfulness was that only one sharp shower was experienced and the pro- gramme was carried out in its entirety. Several noted athletes put in an appearance and there were some keen contests which delighted the spectators. The bicycle handicaps were exception- ally interesting. T. Sherratt, the international, was among the competitors, but in the mile race he was too heavily handicapped even to win his heat. The two mile race gave him a better opportunity of making up the leeway and he displayed such wonder- ful staying powers that he captured the first prize. There were several spills, which detracted a little from the keenness of the struggle, but nothing serious occurred. Local athletes again figured prominently and gave a good account of themselves, the victories of W. Houghton, Warrington, and L. F. Woods, Sankey H., who were second in the mile and two mile bicycle races respectively, being very p 'pular. A new feature this year was the race for tradesmen, shop assistants, and tramway employees which attracted a large number of entries. As an experiment, the competition was encouraging, although in some heats the racing was very poor. As usual the military tournament by a squad of soldiers from Orford Barracks gave consider- able pleasure. The men went through a series of drills and exercises with commendable smartness and the display reflected credit on Sergeants-Instructors Knight and Leach who directed operations. A unique acrobatic display was given by Waldeck Piermont who has come over from Amsterdam and astonished the crowd with his wonderful feats. The Blue Coat. School band was in attendance and, under the con- ductorship of Mr. Armstrong, played enjoyable selec- tions. The arrangements foe the meeting were in every way admirable and great praise is due to the officials who discharged their duties most ably. They were :—President : Mr. M. Nicholls, Chief Constable; Vice-President, Detective Superintendent Monaghan. Judges : Alderman D. Tinnion, J.P., Messrs. Harvey, E. Warren, J. Rabbitt, D. Wyllie, F. Barber, Pick- avance and Booth N.C.U. Timekeeper: Mr. Thomas Locker. Marksmen : Messrs. F. Crabtree, Birchall, W. Carter, Reece, F. Whittaker, J. Beardsall, P. Coates. Stewards: Inspectors Taylor and Smith, Sergeants Williamson and Barlow. Detective Ideacock, Act. Sergt. Stewart. P.C.'s Clough, Hayes, Tolley and Wyse. Clerks of the Course: Chief Inspector Phillips and P. S. Hodgkiss. Starter: Mr. T. Wain- ' wright, Salford Hullers. Secretary: Sergeant W. Evans. Assistant Secretary: P. C. ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" Lucky Liza." Merriment holds sway at the Royal Court Theatre this week, the attraction being the musical absurdity "Lucky Liza." The piece was acclaimed by a crowded house on Monday and the succeeding per- formances have been very largely attended. As an absurdity" Lucky-Liza" makes no pretensions to elaborate plots and the humour is not of the subtle variety, but for breezy, rollicking fun, the play would be hard to beat. It is one long round of hilarity. "Lucky Liza" is a "slavey"who has her own way and takes a delight in making things lively. Of course, she eventually comes into a fortune and is much sought after, but before this she has a breach of promise action against one Biffins, a gardener. The trial is one of the most diverting features of the play, the Judge not being • proof against the fascinations of the plaintiff and awarding her substantial damages. As" Lucky Liza" Miss Madge Brownie is vivacity personified. Her songs are very pleasing and she is a remarkably clever dancer. Mr. Dickie Clare, the manager of the company, scores a huge success in the three parts of Silas Slim, the Judge and the French waiter. Widely varied as the roles are, he is equally at home in each, and keeps the audience in continual mirth. He gives some capital songs, the most popular having for its theme the once familiar ditties which are seldom heard now. There is some excellent dancing, the La Belle Trio being particularly accom- plished in the art. NEXT WEEK'S ATTRACTION.

Next week the beautiful moCier-ti-i;;;;;M•ance "The Love that Women Desire " will be presented by Mr. (I-. Carlton Wallace's Company who pay a return visit. The play is a most enthralling one and should attract crowded audiences. Seats may be booked at the theatre from 11 to 1 daily. . ,

PEN KETH.

FIDDLERS FERRY SAILING AND BOATING CLUB.— On Saturday in a race in connection with the Tran- mere Sailing Club the Fag (Frankish and Gallon) was successful in securing the first prize, a silver cup. pre- sented by the vice-Commodore,Mr. R. Ritchmough. The Ferry Club are doing well,this being the third cup they ig.ve brought home this season. BIBLE CLAss.—The Penketh Wesleyan Men's Bible Class held their open afternoon service on Sunday in the large tent that had been erected for the purpose of the Penketh,Sankey and District. Garden and Cage Bird Society's annual show which was held on Saturday. There was a good attendance, which numbered about, 300. The president, Mr. C. E. Parker took the chair and a very edifying address was delivered by Mr. J. Travers, of Widnes. During ;:the, service the Penketh Tannery Prize Bind under the conductorship of Mr. Harper of Widnes, played selections and the Penketh and Sankey Vocal Union under the conductorship of Mr. D. Dunn, of Sankey Bridges, sang two pieces. viz., "Lead kindly Light" and " Comrades song of Hope." The selections were rendered in a way that redounds much to their credit as they have only been formed so short a time.

EXAMINATION RESULTS. Oxford —Local Lists

WARRINGTON SUCCESSES.

There are numerous local successes in the lists of results of the Oxford Local Examinations which have been published this week. The total number of candi- dates examined was 18,514, as compared with 15,966 last year, and 2,990 certificates have been awarded as against 1,568 in 1906. The greatest numerical advance was among the senior candidates, who numbered 6,370 as against 4,614 last year. The juniors totalled 8,327, an increase of 357 on 1906. The Preliminary candidates last yea: numbered 1,622, this year the total has gone up to 3,817. Of the Seniors 4,045 passed, and 5,887 Juniors and 3,058 Preliminary cacclidates were awarded certificates. The premier position in the first class of the Seniors is gained by J.A. Thorne, Blundell's School, Tivertnn. In the oral examination, 71 candidates passed in French and three in German. In the Junior division E. Jones, Bury Grammar School is placed at the top of the list. In the oral examination 62 candidates passed in French and six in German. WHITE CITY, MANCHESTER.

The fame of the White City of Manchester, and its numerous attractions, has spread all over Great Britain, and in fact all over Europe, and not without good reason, for this electrical city, (as it may be aptly so termed) is indeed a wonderland with attractions at every turn certain to please. Visitors go there day after day and bring their little ones, and others visit this greatest of England's Recreation Parks night after night, always passing a few delightful hours. The attractions are so great in quality and so numerous that space will only allow us to adequately enumerate in full. One can find enjoyment in watching the Free Circus, or listening to the music of the Bands (who play daily afternoon and evening on the spacious Band Stand). At 2.30 in the afternoon the Water Pantomime. given by the so-named "Water Rats of the White City," is one of the features in the list of Free Attractions.' The Most Magnificent Fairy Fountain in the world plays at intervals. At night the City with its 40,000 electric lights makes a peerless display, and is indeed a Fairyland when the evening shadows have fallen upon the busy town. Another special attractions has just been added.—Zipho in his Death-defying Slide for Life on a telegraph wire over 500 feet long, from a height of 150 feet. This is Zipho's first engagement in Great Britain. - •

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

POACHING NEAR RUNCORN.

Two Prodsham men, named Thomas Allman and Albert Pover, were summoned to Runcorn Petty Sessions, on Monday for poaching on land belonging to Mr. A. Harvey Talbot, J.P., of Aston Hall. The men were caught working lan4 with two dogs. Allman was fined 10s. and costs, and Pover ss. and costs.

SWINE FEVER IN CHESHIRE. RUNCORN TRADESMAN FINED.

On Monday, at the Runcorn Police Court, William Ackerley, greengrocer, Church-street, pleaded guilty to making a false declaration for the removal of a pig, and also to removing a pig contrary to the swine fever regulations of the county.—Charles Wilson, of Elles- mere-street,also pleaded guilty to causing the pig to be removed, but was allowed to withdraw the plea. The circumstances, as related by Superintendent Ennion. were that Wilson asked Ackerley to remove a pig to Halton for him. At five o'clock on Monday morning Ackerley obtained two declaration forms, and half an hour later Police-sergeant Allonbo met Ackerley remov- ing the pig, asked for tile declarations, and was handed the blank forms. He sent him back to get the forms filled in by Wilson, but Ackerley filled them in himself. because Wilson was in bed. Ackerley was fined 40s. and costs, 12s. for making the false declaration, and 10s. and costs 9s. 6d. for removing the pig. Wilson was also fined 106. and costs. The magistrates' clerk (NIL John- son) told Ackerley he had made himself liable to a penalty of £40.-9trperintendent Eonion said he had received a communication from the Chief Constable that swine fever was spreading in the county, and all steps must be taken to put it down.

The Parish Council of Walton Inferior sent a com- munication that it had been reported to them that the corner of :11r. Bainbridge's garden at the junction of Walton and Hill Cliffe roads is dangerous considering the amount of motor traffic and they desired the District Council to consider the advisability of forming a circular corner at this point. . _

- The Surveyor suggested that the wall be lowered. The Chairman: It is a most dangerous corner. Mr. Hallows: It is the most dangerous corner in the Stockton Heath neighboorhood. There have been several narrow escapes. The Surveyor was instructed to r3port further to the Highways Committee.

DA RESBURY SEWERAGE.

Colonel John Clare Ridgway, of Daresbury Lodge, under date 29th July, wrote complaining of sewage contamination of an open ditch from the Daresbury tank and asking that the drain from the tank should be connected with his drainage pipes, as he s..rongly objected to his place being spoiled by sewage. The Clerk reported that since Colonel Ridgway's death his son had written confirming the letter. Mr. Farrington said he had interviewed Mr. Ridgway and assnrred him that the Council would no doubt be willing to do all they could to abate a nuisance if any existed. Mr. Ridgway had agreed to take observa- tions and if he found anything suspicious to let him know. In his (Mr. Farrington's) opinion the contamina- tion was from the road washings which contained lime. Dr. Adams said that personally he considered the effluent from the tank was satisfactory but he suggested that full bacteriological examinations should be made. This was agreed to. NORLEY WATER QUESTION.—A BREEZE. _

Mr. Reginald Potts, clerk to the Cheshire County Council, wrote that in March last the County Analyst analysed a sample of water taken from the pump well to a new house in Cow-lane, named Manor House, in the parish of Norley. The analyst certified that the nitrates were very high, showing previous sewage con- tamination, and the Health Committee of the County Council requested the District Council to take steps to prevent the use of such water for domestic purposes. Mr. Woodward said the Norley Water Committee met that morning, and was informed that at a recent parish meeting Norley promised to amend one of the public water supplies as an experiment. The Committee was not satisfied with that, as the question not only touched the comparatively few public supplies in the village, but a great number of private supplies. The Com- mittee believed that if a poll were taken the majority of the inhabitants would be in favour of the full scheme, and they recornlnended that it be carried out in accordance with plans already submitted to the Local Government Board. Mr. Linaker seconded and the recommendation was agreed to with one dissentient. It was decided to advertise for tenders.

Mr. Bell said he wished to protest against the dis- graceful way in which the medical officer had treated him. Three months ago he (Mr. Bell) asked Mr. Farrington and Dr. Adams some questions. Mr. Farlington kindly answered him at the next meeting but Dr. Adams had put him off with excuses and had not answered the questions yet. He wanted the ques- tions to be answered in writing for the next meeting. Dr. Adams said that speaking purely as an official he did not consider it was the right of any District Councillor to demand answers to questions from him of a personal, private or other character which were not under the authority of the chair. He maintained that his report of July Bth contained a full answer to every one of Mr. Bell's questions. He apologised to Mr. Bell for any sense of discourtesy ho might have felt, and assured him it had never been meant. Mr. Bell was not the only person in Norley who had approached him and he had turned to them all the same cheek of non- cornplial. That was the only attitude he could main- tain unless the Council thought he should sink the dignity of his office in a matter of personalities. He was not the servant of any District Councillor bat of the District Council.

Mr. Bell persisted in his questions, and after further discussion the Chairtnan ruled that as Dr. Adams said his report covered the questions, he (the Chairman) could not interfere in the matter.

It was agreed that Mr. Ball should be supplied with a copy of the report.

A TRAMP IS TROUBLE.

Fair Chambers, a tramp, was charged at the Warring- ton Occasional Court on Thursday with stealing from the house 117, Penny-lane, Haydock, one coat, a wooden pipe and a key the property of Mary Ann Smales of the above address. Prisoner was seen by a neighbour to go in the lobby and take the coat off the clothes rail id the lobby.—lnformation was received by P.C. Taylor who accordingly arrested Chambers.—Thomas Davies manager for Messrs. Sutton and Sons, pawnbrokers, 47, Gerard-street, Ashton-in-Makerfield, said prisoner, who gave his name as John Jackson, came to him in the pledge office where prisoner asked for 3s. on the coat. He advanced him 2.5. 3d.—Mary Ann Smales said the property belonged to her husband and it was worth 15s.—Prisoner was convicted to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions. WOULD NOT PAY. At the County Petty Sessions hold at Warrington before Captain Withington and other justices on Wednesday, James Jameson was summoned for arrears of mainten- ance to the amount of 46 is. dne to his wife, Jane Jameson, living at Earlestown, who bad had to apply to the Guardians for relief.—Superintendent Innes said that Jameson told him before the Court that he would not pay. lie was a very good workman, but he was out of work at present. They had great difficulty in finding him. They had a three days' search.—Prisoner, who said he could not pay, was sent to gaol for two calendar months with hard labour. 'MEANT TARING IT BACK."

Lavinia Watson of no settled abode was charged on remand at Warrington on Wednesday with stealing on the 6th inst., a blanket of the the value of 10s. 6d., the property of Benjamin Barker, Haydock-street, Earles- town.—May Barker, daughter of prosecutor, said the prisoner was working at their house for a fortnight. On the date mentioned the blanket was on the bathroom table but later in the day she missed it. At 6.30 p.m she saw prisoner, who had been for her clothing, leaving with two parcels under her arm.—John Morrow said he , was manager for Messrs. Callendar's, pawnbrokers, Earlestown. The prisoner, who gave the name of May Watson, Hotel-street, Earlestown, came in the shop with the blanket for which, as she had asked him to, he gave her 2s. Gd. P.C. Lennie said when he charged prisoner with the theft she said" I did not steal it on purpose, I meant taking it back with my wages this week." He received her from the Leigh police. She was fined 10s. including costs, or seven days imprison- ment.

CATHOLIC YOUNG MEN'S SOCIETY.

IThe members of the above society held their first smoking concert at their new quarters, in Fairclough- street on Saturday. The principal artistes who came from St. Helens were assisted by local talent tinder the direction of Mr. T. A. Dennett. In introducing the artistes to the audience, the President (Mr. Cosgrove) reminded the members, that although every effort was being made by the committee to minister to the requirements both in the way of refreshments and amusements, they must still keep before them the fact that they belonged to a religious society. He urged them to attend earnestly and regularly to their funda- mental obligations. The programme, which lasted over two hours, was an exceedingly good one. All the artistes were well received and all the items were well rendered. At the close Mr. E. Fitzgerald proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Denuett, the organizer of the concert, for his efforts in getting together such a talented company, and expressed the hope that this concert would be the forerunder of several similar ones. Mr. W. F. Hurst (secretary) seconded, and Councillor Toole (president of the Warrington Society) 'supported the motion. The vote was carried with acciamation,and Mr. Dennett responded.

fIAYDCCK COLLIERY SICK CLUB. BIG MAJORITY FOR WINDING-UP.

The men belonging to tbe sick club connected with Messrs. Evans and Co's. collieries at Haydock, St. Helens, Ashton, Edge Green, and Golborne have decided upon winding up the club. It is announced that out of a vote of 4,500 members the majority is over two to one for terminating the club. The Society has been in existence since 1862, and contributions have been at the request of the men, collected from the firm's cashier, and the practical management has been carried out by the firm's representatives. At the annual meeting a fortnight ago it was announced that there was a debt of close on £4OO. A meeting was held at the Waggon and Horses Hotel, 'Elaydock, last week, of delegates from the whole of the committees connected with the branches of the Miners' Federation at the various pits of Messrs. R. Evans and Company to consider the question of the formation of a new sick club in place of the :dub which has been carried on for so many years by the firm.— Mr. T. Walton presided, and it was explained that as the old sick club has ceased to provide either benefits or medical attendance it was urgently necessary that a new club should be formed.— The meeting after long consideration, decided that an amalgamated sick and funeral club be formed for every pit and workshop under Messrs R. Evans and Company including surface and underground workers. The con- tributions were fixed at 4d. per week,but female workers and all under 16 of age years are so be called upon to pay half. The benefits of the new club are to be Bs. full pay for six months, and 2s. for a further twelvemonths, and in the event of death £3 per member, and £4 on the death of a member's wife. The new society will take up all the liabilities of all members of the old sick club, for sick cases only, but claims will be taken up from the griginal date and on the new scale. It was decided that each branch should hold a special meeting, and that the delegates should meet on Tuesday next to draw up a code of rules. In respect of accident all the men connected with the firm are pressed to join the new permanent relief society, so as to cover them- selves in cases of accident. Men who do not belong to either the new or old permanent club are at present left without medical attendance in the case of accidents. Until the 23rd inst. the committee have decided to pro- vide medical attendance for all who may, under such circumstances, meet with accidents; but after that date the committee will take no responsibility.—The medical officers whe will act for the new society in the case of accident are Dr. Dowling, Haydock ; Dr. O'Keefe, St. Helens; Dr. Mapei, Golborne ; Dr. Mather, Billinge ; and Dr. Latham, Ashton.

ST. HELENS.

BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Mr. W. S. Dromgoole was on Monday returned unopposed as a representative of the Hardshaw Ward of St. Helens on the Prescot Board of Guardians, in place of the late Mr. R. D. Houghton. CHEMICAL WORKS INVADED. Three rough-looking men, named Thomas Houghton, James Ashcroft, and Josepix Kerfoot, were at St. Helens on Saturdas ordered to find sureties or go to gaol for fourteen days for invading the Cliire Chemical Works the previous afternoon and threatening the workmen. The works watchman said that owing to the conduct of the three men the workmen had to go out and leave their work, and then the prisoners began fighting one another, and Kerfoot was badly mauled. ST, HELENS SHOPKEEPER FINED.

At St. Helens, on Saturday, Guy Kelly, small ware dealer, of Duncan-lane, was charged with exposing for sale obscene picture post-cards. Inspector Jackson proved the case, and said that under a search warrant he seized sixty-one cards which were indecent.—Mrs. Kelly appeared, and said that the same cards were exhibited for sale all over St. Helens. She was simply doing what other shopkeepers were doing. They had not a single indecent card in their shop, and if they were suggestive it was only to prurient minds.—The Chairman said defendant took a very high-handed course in not appearing before the court, and in Mrs. I Kelly stating that this kind of card was to be seen in all the shops of St. Helens. That was not so, and she had no right to make such statements.—Mrs. Kelly asked to see the cards in order to be sure that they were the ones that had come from her shop, and afterwards she said they were simply fat figures and nothing more. Any person would look the same if they were costumed like that. The magistrate ordered the cards to be destroyed, and defendant to pay 12s. costs., ST. HELENS CLERK'S TRAGIC DEATH.

Mr. Brighouse, at Eccleston,on Monday, inquired into the death of Mr. 'John Wilson, aged fifty-eight, the chief pay clerk at Messrs. Pilkington's sheet-glassworks He had been secretary of the St. Helens recreation. club for over 25 years, and was found drowned on Thursday morning last in the Mill Data brook at liccleston. The coroner and jury viewed the scene of the tragedy. Mrs. swift, wife of Alfred Swift, formerly of Springfield Farm, Eccleston, identi- fied the deceased as her father. hie resided at 98, Norwood-road Southport, but for the past two years he had lived with them during the week, and had gone home at the week-ends. He was somewhat weak in his walking, and on the morning prior to his death he sat down suddenly while washing himself. She asked him what was the matter, and he said," I feel done up. I wish my holidays were on." He had never at any time threatened to take his life. On Thursday morning he left home after breakfast as usual to go to the works. Edward John Hickman, insurance agent, of Gillers Green, said that he was going along the footpath towards St. Helens on Thursday morning, at half-past eight, when a nurse named Mabel Cullaford pointed to something in the water. They pulled the body out and found it was Mr. Wilson. There was only a single rail fence along the footpath side, but where the body was the rail was missing. The foot- path was worn away by the water, and there were large pools of water on the surface, which made the path very slippery. It would be quite possible for deceased, when stepping over a pool of water, to have slipped into the brook. He had noticed deceased stagger as he walked, and he appeared far from strong.—Police- constable Young corroborated as to the state of the footpath. The ditch was eight feet wide, and the water over three feet deep. In the present state of the foot- path and fence it was dangerous to the travelling public. A feeble person might easily slip in.— Fred Atherton, gamekeeper at Eccleston Hall, said he found deceased's umbrella sticking upright in the brook as if he bad put it out to save himself when he slipped in.—Mr. Dickinson, solicitor to Messrs. Pilkington, expressed the sympathy of the firm for the family. Deceased had been in their service over thirty years— from 1876—and during that long term of service he had done his duty faithfully and well. Everyone _connected with the firm felt deceased's tragic death very much.— The jury returned a verdict that deceased was accident- ally drowned,and expressed the opinion that the present condition of the footpath and ditch was dangerous to the travelling public, and the authority having legal control over the same ought to take immediate steps to remedy the present state of affairs.

A WOULD-BE PATTI

BY RAMA WEST,

Author of "From Across the Sea," "An Aft thought," "The One Hope," "A Double IM, take," "On the Wings of the Morning," &c

"An easy run, good roads,. pleasant coun,4-y inn where you eon recruit at your lcis.‘ure alter tt-le fatigues of the journey, two pretty girls to r.nt,r- tain you as soon as you feel up to being enter- s worth thinking about, Kenningham Gilbert Kenningham stretched his long lbebs in lazy enjoyment of the warmth from crackling wood fire, for the evening was ra, chill, thoogh it was the mithile of the rnerrit, month of v.

"I snupose- some sort of a chern.-Te is rood for one son), times, In urn-inured, refl. vr! "And, Nvhat your sister says, I iiriagiiie the Mi.s Farrel's will not ohject to cat-i- -t:tin strangers unawares, provided, or course; rlify .are welt known to their rhoperon ? Is that N%.ilA :Miss Vynor considers hersrlf " "I am sure I don't lfnow what she considers huself," Fred Vynor replied, in tones whose briskness centraste6 oddly with his eemnanion's leisurely drawl. "It is wi:AL she is, as far as I ,can make our. Julia was always given to keeping her own counsel, hut, from all I have gathercd, the facts in a nutshell are these: She wanted to live in a big house with luxurious surroundings; but, though she is comfortably off, her income would not run to her own carriage and a staff of servants. The Farrell girls were on the look out :for a lady companion, they having had a split with the aunt who had been living with them since their parents' death. Julia has been there over a twelvemonth, so I suppose they are used to each other by now, and I believe she made same sort of a stipulation as to being able to invite her friends occasionally. Make your mind easy; we shall have the entrée to Wychford House whenever we desire it."

"And you have never seen either of our future hostesses ? "

"No. Julia and I are not devotedly attached to each other, and for nine months of the time she has been with the Farrells they have been travelling abroad, so I could not have gone to 'see her even had I wished to do so. Now she wants to talk over business matters, that is why .she is anxious for me to go down soon. Though she is given to lecturing and ordering me about as if I were still in jackets, she does give me •credit for a clear head for business, and actually condescends to ask my opinion on that score. As I am bound to go, I thought I might as well combine pleasure with business and spin down on my machine; then it occurred to me that you needed rousing up, and, as I am about the only person who ever succeeds in that difficult opera- tion, I determined to rout you out and get you to join me." Gilbert nodded. "You certainly are the only person I ever allow to worry me into abnormal activity; but this time I must confess your pro- gramme sounds attractive, so I'm your man!" Three days later he and Fred rode up to the Wychford Arms, which fully justified the latter'. ,description—"a pleasant country inn." "That's active:exereise enough for the present," Gilbert said as he followed his friend into the house. "That last hill was terrible. I feel "quite done up with such unwonted exertion, so while you interview .Miss Vynor I shall indulge in a pipe and a book under the trees. I am afraid your afternoon devoted to the discussion of stocks and shares will not be quite so pleasant." Fred laughed. "Business interviews with Julia are not pleasant as a rule, I must confess," he said. "She wants everything settled her own way, and gets vexed because her way usually bristles with impossibilities. But it's no use postponing the evil moment, so I will go at once and leave you to smoke in peace. I shall get back as soon as I possibly can." And, having inquired the exact whereabouts of the Farrells' house, he set forth on his errand. It was a long, •straggling village, and Wychford House, he had been told, was some little distance from the other end of it. His nearest way would be across the fields and down the lane separating the grounds of Wychford Hall. and House respectively. The weather had changed, the sun being now so powerful that he was thankful to get into the shade of the trees after the hot •walk across the open fields. "I suppose I am going right," he muttered, •doubtfully, 4but it seems the deuce of a dis- tance. Why, whatever's that? Some animal in awful pain, I should think, poor beast! but it's the eueerest noise I ever heard." He searched carefully round, but could find nothing, and the noise having ceased after a last prolonged wail. lie concluded that death had mercifully put an -end to the creature's sufferings, and was going on his way, when through an open gate he espied the figure of a girl standing beneath the spreading branches of a big hawthorn. From her attitude .and the style of her dress, she evidently flattered 'herself on resembling one of Rosetti's ideals, but she flattered herself in vain. The mouth -certainly suggested Rosetti ; but the eyes, set far :apart, were insignificant; the nose faulty, com- plexion drab-white. Her abundant hair was parted low to touch the eyebrows, and, covering the tops of the ears, was gathered in a knot, low down in the neck. It was heartless, perhaps, to disturb the entraneed rapture into which she appeared to have fallen, but the afternoon was on the wane, and he much wanted to find out if he was going in the right direction, so approach- ing to within a few yards of the motionless figure he made his inquiry. A shade of annoy- ance crossed her face at the sound of his voice; then, slowly raising her long, thin, hand she pointed up the lane. " But will you kindly tell me if it is much .:further ? " No answer.

"She can't be deaf, because she understood .the first time," he thought, repeating the ques- tion in a louder tone. Still no answer. A third attempt. _ _ _ _ _

This time the girl pointed to her mouth. "Dumb, poor thing! but there seems nobody ,else about to ask, so I had better write the question down. If the house is much further -UAW, I must nut off seeinir Julia until to-

Scribbling a few lines on the back of an envelope he handed it and his pencil to the girl, but to his astonishment she waved him aside with an im- perious gesture, and, again ' pointing down the lane, deliberately turned her back on him. "She must be crazy," he said to himself. "Well, at any rate, this long lane must come to an end soon, so I may as well go on. His patience was soon rewarded by the sight of a house nestling amongst the trees, and before many minutes had passed his sister was greeting him with so much more than her usual cordiality that the thought crossed his mind "She wants me to do something special for her." He was not long kept in suspense. Contrary to his advice she had been induced to take shares in a company which had since given ominous signs of failure, and now wanted his opinion as to what she had better do.

"Whether I sell out out or keep in, it means my income being considerably lessened, I see that right enough," she said, drawing her dark, finely-pencilled eyebrows together in a frown of perplexity. "I frankly own I have been wrong, utterly wrong, and you 'Were perfectly right" ("She must be hard presed to own that," he thought). "But having got into the trouble, the thing is how best to get out of it. I don't want to leave here, hut on the other hand I can't stop on with a much smaller income, for I am bound to dress well, and keep up a certain position. No," as he strove to get in a few words, "I cannot borrow from you. I appreciate your kindness in making the offer, but whatever I do I won't be unjust. I have got myself into the mess, and I will get myself out of it, without dragging you into the quicksands of temporary poverty. Just run through these figures, will you, and tell me exactly how I shall stand." The result was not satisfactory, but before there was time to discuss the question in all its bear- ings they were interrupted by the arrival of tea and tea-maker—the younger Miss Farrell, Ida. She was an extremely pretty, fair-haired, lively girl of nineteen, and, though Julia evidently awaited her exit with ill-concealed impatience, Fred felt as if the sunshine had been suddenly blotted out, when, after inviting himself and his friend to join them at luncheon on the morrow, she left the room, saying that after so long an absence she was sure Miss Vynor must wish to have a long talk with her brother. "What a fascinating little being," he ex- claimed, as he watched her run down the terrace steps on to the lawn. "Oh my dear Fred, has not time cured you cl falling in love with every passably pretty face you meet ? " Julia said. Then, as if recollecting herself, she began asking him about his journey down. She laughed when he described his encounter with the damsel beneath the hawthorn. "That was Henrietta Farrell—Henriette, as she insists on calling herself. She is always picking up nesv fads, and her latest is to imagine her,,elf a second Adelina Patti. Patti never talks more than is necessary for some little time before she is going to sing, consequently Henriette, the day she intends performing at the concerts held in the village schoolroom, maintains an almost unbroken silence." "Then she is not dumb ? " "Dumb? No. There are moments when I devoutly wish she were. As you can guess from her style of dress and general appearance the fad before was the Rosetti cult. Heaven knows what lc next will be ! " "But is her voice so beautiful ? "

Julia shrugged her shapely shoulders. "I will leave you to form your own opinion, for you and Mr. Kenningham will most certainly have to put in an appearance this evening." "Will you be there ? " _ _ _

"Most -assuredly. I could not possibly let the two girls go alone. By the way, what kind of a man is Mr. Kenningham ? " "The best fellow in the world if only he wa.l not so intolerably lazy. However, as he is one of the lucky ones, born with enough to keep up a luxurious home without the trouble of working for it, his laziness does not so much matter. He is awfully tender-hearted; gets taken in right and ieft because he cannot bring himself to give pain to a soul."

"Ah! There is such a thing as being too easy- going. Why does he not and a wife to look after him ? " "I verily believe because he is too lazy to take the trouble of wooing her," replied Fred, laugh- ing. "But this is not settling your affairs," drawing the papers towards him as he spoke.

After- another half-hour spent in frying to solve the problem of restoring order to Miss Vynor's money matters he rose to go, promising to do his utmost to induce Gilbert to accompany him that evening. As he went down the avenue he met Ida coming towards the house, and stopped to ask her what time the concert began, Julia having omitted to tell him. "I am glad you are going," the girl said, frankly. "For talking is permitted during the performance of the drum and fife band, and I must confess I get heartily tired of these d:eadful evenings for the people'. Henrietta gets so excited over singing herself that she hardly notices anything else, but Miss Vynor and I find it fearfully slaw. We are bound to go, because now the Wychford Hall people are away we have to do double duty in taking an interest in local entertainments."

"Your sister is very fond of music, I suppose ? " Fred said, prolonging the interview so as to admire at his leisure the varying expression of the piquant little beauty at his side. "Ye—es," said Ida, dubiously. "She says her whole soul is/wrapped in it. I think myself that while we were abroad it is a pity she did not take some good singing lessons, but she says the only way to sing is to emit perfectly natural sounds, and that a good voice is spoilt from over cultivation. Ah, there she is! We had better -walk on a little, 'Mr. Vynor, for she greatly dislikes being spoken to on the days she is going to sing." Fred felt inclined to repeat his former verdict of "crazy," as he watched Miss Farrell moving slowly in their direction, the clinging folds of her pale yellow gown dragging limply behind her.

"She would not be bad-looking if only she were less affected," he thought. "But as she is --Heaven help lie man who has a wife like that!"

After some difficulty he induced Gilbert to sacrifice himself on the shrine of duty, and they rnached the door of the concert room just as the Fareells' carriage drove up. Ida, looking more charming than ever in evening dress, led the way to some front seats; Henriette, arrayed in a wonderful trailing garment of sage green and gold, disappearing into one of the class-rooms to await her turn to sing. "SI-43 always does that until after her first song," Ida informed him under cover of the din made by the drum and fife band which opened the proceedings. "Because it is so bad for the voice to breathe the air of a heated room. If possible she always practises out of doors. Her song is next on the programme, for she is to sing three times to-night.."

Half-way- through that first song Fred, remembering what Ida had told him about practising in the open air, began to suspect the cause of the strange sounds he had believed to be the dying agonies of some wounded animal. The final high note removed all doubt. It might be natural—it certainly was excruciating. Ida kept her eyes steadily bent on her programme, and began asking if he and Mr. Kenningham cared for golf, for the Wychford links were con- sidered excellent, and as they intended staying a week they might be glad to know of them. Fred, seeing her evident wish to avoid discussing her sister's performance, took the hint and began discussing golf with ardour. It was far too much exertion for Gilbert, but he himself was an enthusiast, and would gladly accompany Ida the following morning if she would allow him to be her companion. The next five days passed quickly, he and she constantly :together on the links, Gilbert walking and driving with Julia and Henriette.

"If you're not careful, old man," Fred said to hint th.e eveninw before her departure- "Nig.'

"carrell's next fall will be—marriage.'

"What do you mean ? " murMured Gilbert, sleepily. "That she has taken a fancy to you, and is uncommonly anxious to cultivate your acquaint- ance."

"I really think you are mistaken. I have done nothing to make her imagine for one moment that I—er—admire her in the least."_

"Perhaps not; but all the same she considers that you and she have strong affinities. She told mo so herself."

"Can't say I feel that way; quite the eon- t-ary. But it does not much matter, for we leave to-morrow' and as she is evidently a damsel of many moods my image will doubtless quickly fade from her mind."

But Fate willed him to be a prisoner at the Wyehford Arms for many a day to come. That very night he slipped on the highly- polished stairs, with results that made it im- possible for him to be moved further than the sofa till the London season was nearly at its close. He took the weary time of convalescence contentedly enough, but Fred felt distinctly uneasy. True, running down to see his friend gii.ve him delightful opportunities of also seeing Ida, who always welcomed him with a cordiality that made him indeed hope that in time their friendship might ripen into a warmer feeling; but Henriette spent far less time in practising, nd frequently accompanied Miss Vynor to cheer he solitary confinement of that "Poor dear Mr. lieuningham, who bore his sufferings with the patience of an angel." When at last business called him abroad, and he could no longer play the part of watch-dog, he determined in despera- tion to confide in his sister.

"Do for goodness' sake put a stop to Miss Farrell's frequent visits," he pleaded. "Gilbert's exactly the sort of fellow to get entangled in an en,:agement. He will make himself pleasant and agreeable, and never notice that what he looks upon as mere civilities are being taken seriously. 1 hen all of a sudden he will wake up to the fact that the girl expects him to propose to her, and from .sheer want of energy to put a stop to the whole proceeding he will just drift into an unhappy marriage. Think what marriage with a girl like Miss Farwell would mean to any intelli- gent man. Help me to save Gilbert from that, Julia, or I shall never forgive myself for having bought him down here." Miss Vynor laughed. "You forget my duties as chaperon," she said. "Mr. Kenningham is clever, of good family, handsome, rich—a most eligible parti in spite of his being on the shady side of fifty. Why should I try and stop what would certainly be an excellent match for Henriette? I am by no means blind, and see clearly how matters are tending between you and Ida; yet, as you know, I have placed no obstacles in the way, though, my dear Fred, you are by no means so desirable a suitor as Mr. Kenningham, his twenty added years notwithstanding." "Then you refuse to help me ?" he said, ignor- ing the last remark. "I intend to let things take their course,' replied Miss Vynor. "And that reminds me, I promised to escort Henriette to your friend'e this afternoon. She has nromised to while away the weary hours by singing to the accompaniment of her guitar." "The last straw!" ejaculated Fred. "The singing when somebody else plays is bad enough; when she plays herself it must be agonising. And I did want some golf to-day; still, I feel tt my duty to support Gilbert as long as I can, so I will endure the torture manfully. Cut it as short as you can, Julia." "You may be sure I will, for my own sake," returned his sister, drily. Fred employed the interval which elapsed before the torture commenced by urging his friend to firmly withstand the wiles of the fair Henriette. Gilbert seemed languidly amused. "Barring the voice she is not so bad," he said. "She has read a good deal in a desultory fashion, travelled, is used to society. Of course, she is spoilt by being so affected; but, shut up as I am here, even that makes a change. I am always wondering what will be the mkt fad." "You for a husband, if you don't look out," began Fred, impatiently, but broke off as Miss Vynor and her charges came in. Fred brightened visibly at the sight of Ida, whom he promptly ensconced by the window as far as possible away from the high-backed chair Henriette selected for herself. It did not add to his peace of mind to see that Miss Farrell, in her trailing, terra- cotta colour robe looked picturesque, almost pretty, as she adjusted her guitar and began to play. It also disturbed him to find that her playing was fairly good. Gilbert, under pretext of wishing to take a lesson, delayed the songs as long as possible, a manceuvre which greatly edified Miss Vynor. Fred, watching his apparent interest as the girl leant over his couch shewing him how to manage the instrument, wished from the bottom of his heart that business did not call him away on the morrow. "It will be a settled thing by the time I get back," he said to himself, and the idea haunted him through the two or three songs which followed the guitar lesson. "Poor old Gilbert, that row would drive him crazy," he thought, as the shrill voice rasped out an Italian song with many runs and trills.- "He wouldn't have the strength of mind to forbid her to sing, for he hates scenes, and she is as obstinate as a mule; she wouldn't give up her own way for anything, I know that from the life she leads Ida, poor darling. If only I could make Gilbert under- stand! "

During the time he was compelled to spend abroad he wrote regularly to his friend making anxious inquiries as to the progress of affairs, and it disquieted him not a little to find that his questions were carefully ignored. The moment he was free he hurried down to Wychford, where Gilbert was still a prisoner, though considerably better. The first person he saw on leaving the station was his sister.

"I thought you would come by this train," she said. "And as I rather wanted to speak to you before you met Mr. Kenningham I came to greet you. He did not want to write the news, but a few days ago he was engaged to be married." Fred groaned. "Poor Gilbert—l pity him from my heart! He is worthy of a better. fate," he said, despondingly. "It's all my fault; it was I who induced him to come in the first instance. Married—and to such a woman!"

"You are not very complimentary to me, dear," Julia said, with a little laugh. " You ? What have you got to do with it ? " "A good deal, considering I am going to marry him."

Fred stared at her blankly. "But I thought--" he began: "That Gilbert was Henriette's latest fad—that, in short, she shewed herself by no means averse to becoming his partner for life. He was an interlude. She has picked up a German pro- fessor since, old enough and ugly enough to make him a perfectly safe pastime. He flatters her to the top of her bent; says her voice is 'Perfectly bea-u-ti-ful ' (I wonder the barefaced lie does not choke him), but sadly needs cultivation. She implicitly believes him, and as he comes down four times a week from town to superintend the process of cultivation, you can see that he has his eyes wide open to the main chance, and will not easily let his pupil escape him. Ida needs consolation (which doubtless you can administer), for the practising goes on more vigorously than ever. I don't think you need pity Gilbert, for I have a strong idea he and I will get on well together. I shall take all trouble off his hands, and, as he says, it really is about time he had someone to look after him."

"But why on earth did you not give me a hint as to the true state of affairs, instead of letting me worry my life out like this ? " "Gilbert said he wanted to take you by surprise. As for me, you could hardly expect me to say anything until he had actually proposed; and that was so short a time ago that we both agreed it would be better to wait and tell you instead of writing. I fancy Henriette was quicker witted than you, and it was when she began to suspect something that she took up with the professor •—out of pique. When we are married—which, by the way, will be soon—l shall make a point of trying to find her a suitable husband; if he is a little deaf it will be so much the better. Mean- while she will be perfectly content to pose na a would-be Patti."

Charge Against South Lancashire Privates.

The two —privates of the 2nd South Lancashire Regi- ment, Joseph Brennan and Thomas Fahey, were com- mitted for trial from Camberley on Tuesday on a charge of causing the death of Frank Devlin, a reservist of the regiment. Devlin was found drowned in the river Blackwater with severe injuries about the body, on August, 3rd, th 3 day on which he was leaving the barracks to take train for Ireland. —A heavy wooden pole was produced in court, and it was alleged that with this instrument Devlin was rolled into the river and held under the water until he was dead.

POLITICIAN. SOCIALISM AND THE TEACHINGS OF CHRIST.

• A few weeks ago, Mr. Charles E. Parker replied through the correspondence columns of the 1" Examiner " to some of my observations and ques- tioned or contradicted most of my findings. Since then until now I have not had time to reply. Mr. Parker is a man of parts. He is (or was) the President of the Warrington Liberal Associaticn and i the President of the Free Church Council. He is! therefore an opponent of some calibre; and to discuss I Politics and Religion with such an one, I contemplate with mingled feelings of pleasure arid awe. But it is not altogether on account of his personal ability and character that I want to cross swords with him. He represents a certain class of thought and disposition; and it is obvious that in meeting him I am confronted also by a large following of individuals who think as he thinks. In the first place, I ought to refresh the minds of my readers as to what the controversy is all about. It will be remembered that in writing a few weeks ago I mentioned the parable of the labourer in the vineyard and tried to show that each man gave accord- ing to his ability and received according to his needs. I used that as evidence that such a doctrine was intended to be taught by Christ. Mr. Parker denied the truth of my interpretation. Each man did not so receive, he said ; the need of each man differs accord- ing to the capacity of his stomach, his brain, the number of his family, &c. He also accused me oinot going to the Scriptures for guidance in the search of truth, but to support some belief I have already adopted. Incidentally, however, I did go to the , 'Scriptures in the search of truth and what is still more surprising I found support to my belief in Socialism. Mr. Parker claims that a man should have the fruit of his labour, the whole fruit, and nothing but the fruit. Socialism claims that each man should give according to his ability and should receive accord- to his needs. The former is a Pagan ideal, Tho latter a Christian. I will explain briefly what I mean. Paganism, we are told, not merely tolerated, but believed in and approved of inequality. It believed also in wealth ; it believed in power ; it believed in war. But all these were repudiated by Christianity. The true Christian is a man who should own nothing; who should turn the other cheek if he were smitten; and offer also his coat, if he were robbed of his cloak. And, so far were early Christians from imagining that those were mere counsels of perfection, that institutions were created and orders founded for putting them into literal practice. The first Christians, when their Church was settled in Jerusalem, lived as Communists. (Communism is an advanced form of Socialism.) We are told in the fourth Chapter of Acts :—" And the multitude of them that believed were of one heart and soul : and not one of them said that aught of the the things which he possessed was his own; but they had all things common. And great grace was, upon them all. For neither was there among them any that lacked; for as many as were possessors of land or houses sold them, and brought the prices of the things that were sold, and laid them at the apostles' feet ; and distribution was blade unto each according as any one had need." From each according to his ability, to each according to his need ! "Not looking each of you to his own things, but each of you also to the things of others." Under the present competitive system, under the system advocated by Mr. Parker, the labourers in the vineyard would have been -paid differently. The "householder," governed by the law of competition, would have given them so much an hour irrespective of their needs. And it may have happened that the man who was engaged last (and who worked 1-mst) had the biggest stomach, most brains, and largest family ; in fact most needs. . Was he to go away hungry, and his family to be neglected, simply because he was not °ire of the first to be selected? There were some who grumbled saying : These last have wrought but one hour, and thou hast made them equal unto us. They believed that they should have received the fruit of their labour, the whole fruit, and nothing but the fruit and because the needs of the others were also con- sidered they kicked against it. They forgot, evidently, the prayer—Give us this day our daily bread— "necessities for all, but harmful luxuries for no one It was not sa much the fact that they were paid a penny that troubled them : it was the thought of the r. others receiving the same. It was the inherent selfish- ness of the individual crying out. His`purpose was to inculcate a-truer, higher, form of human life, that the policy of everyone for himself is not noble and moral • that they should eat to live, and not live to eat, that man should desire not his own well-being alone but equality of well-being. A new scale of values was set up against the old. The Pagan doctrine gave way to the Christian. Everyone will remember the famous anecdote related of Lord Melbourne, how after listening tt, an eloquent but all too practical sermon, he came out of church remarking : "No one has a greater respect than myself for the Church of England. But to bring religion into daily life, is to go a damned sight too . far !" We have it on the authority of a local reverend gentleman that : "On the Stock -Exchange the Jew could discern no difference between the professing Christian and himself in a readiness to take an unfair advantage of another." And what is the reason for such a state of things ? Mr. Parker wants to know whether I would tell him how Socialism, not as a vague ideal merely, but as a definite constructive policy, harmonises with the teachings of Christ. Jesus Christ came into the world and taught, after.. thirty years of preparation, a new type of Divine Human Society : The Kiegdom of Heaven is at hand." The creating of a new society, a new common- wealth of the soul, with new duties, new privileges, and new powers. Man was to give only such attention to secular matters as shall suffice to keep him in life. Seek ye first the Kingdom of God, and his righteous- ness ; and clothing, food and drink shall be added _unto you. Thy Kingdom come, Thy will be done, in earth as in heaven. Ruskin says: When you pray 'Thy Kingdom come,' you either want it to come or you don't. If you don't, you should not pray for it. If you do, you must do more than pray for it —you must live for it, and labour for. the Kingdom of God. , Socialism aims at an Industrial Commonwealth founded upon the Socialism of Land and Capital. The programme of the Independent Labour Party reads thus: The true object of industry being the produc- tion of the requirements of life, the responsibility should rest with the community ccllectively, therefore: The land being the storehouse of all the necessaries of life, should be declared and treated as public property. The capital necessary for industrial operations should be owned and used collectively. Work, and wealth resulting therefrom, should be equitably distributed over the population. As a means to that end it adxocates a maximum a4B hours working week. The provision of work to all capable adult applicants. State pensions for every person over 50 sears of age and adequate provision for all widows, orphans, sick or disabled workers. Free education. The raising of the age of child labour, &c. I should like to ask Mr. Parker whether he thinks there is anything un-Christian in the programme of the 1.L.P., which is a Socialist organization. Place it side by side with the programme of the Liberal party and the Tory party and then let Mr. Parker , judge for himself and tell us which of them I harmonises most with the teachings of Christ. I shall then have something further to say. How is it that the equalising spirit,of Christianity has effected so little in removing the prejudices of employment? And why is it that difference of employment is considered ground enough for men to erect distinction upon? and why are there 12 millions who are living on the verge of starvation? Is there any brotherly love and a helping hand; any care for the weak, the infirm, the old; any Christianity in the• claim that a man'should get the fruit of his labour, the whole fruit, and nothing but the fruit? I hope I am not uncharit ible, but for the life of me I cannot see it. Perhaps Mr. Parker will explain. How much healthier would be the action of the heart, if its life-blood instead of stagnating in its meanest dohs, should, by that great Christian teaching, Love thy neighbour as thyself, be pulsed forth in working each for all and all for each. JOHN WORKMAN.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each, week. THE TRUE SOCIALIST POLICY.

Sir, —Your contributor "Socialist Trades Unionist" in "The Voice of Labour" column of Saturday last unjustifiably exaggerates the political influence wielded by that Socialist organisation known as "The Independent Labour Party" and b:Astfully asserts that "we " (meaning the 1.L.P.) "are seeking the destruction of the Liberal party and we (Oh mighty 1.L.P.) aim at the destruction of the Tory party as well."

In the first place, sir, let me make my position quite clear. I have been for over ten years a convinced Socialist and (incidentally) am also a member of a Trades Union. I firmly believe that a complete re-organisation of our industry and commerce coupled with the socialism of the means of life will alone efficiently solve the Complex unemployed problem or actually remove from our midst the great evils of poverty, destitution, overcrowding, and the various other social diseases from. which, alas, we (as a community) so acutely suffer at present. Let me °here point out to your corre.spondent that these drastic reforms cannot possibly be satisfactorily secured, until they receive the intelligent enthusiastic and continuous support of (at least) a large majority of the electorate of this country. The Liberal and Tory parties (who have jointly received nine-tenths of the recorded votes at recent contested elections),are both opposed to Socialism and officially advocate a continuation of the existing wages system! likewise the Trade Unionist organisations are mainly composed of Liberals and Tories, and there- fore Anti-Socialists.

Still in spite of these facts we read that the I.L.P. are seeking the destruction of both the Liberal and Tory parties, but (according to our local authority) are also desirous of a political alliance with the Trade Unionists (Tories as well as Liberals). I humbly suggest that political experience has certainly proved that these compacts do not necessarily further the true interests of the Socialist movement. Has your correspondent read the recent writings on this subject by such prominent Socialists as Robert Blatchford, Bernard Shaw and Alfred Russell Wallace or is he conversant with the expressed opinion (on this point) of the Father of English Socialism, H. M. Hyndman.

Personally I am not ashamed of my faith and strongly object to subvert my Socialism for the pur- pose of securing the tardy financial support of any Liberal or Tory Trade Unionist. I shall therefore continue to openly and fearlessly propagate those principles, the political application of which will, I believe, enrich, elevate and ennoble human life and ultimately destroy the brutalising influences of com- mercialism, jingoism and wage-slavery.—Yours, etc., COMMONSENSE.

P.S. —By the way, will your correspondent please explain why he thinks "The Independent Labour Party "a suitable title for a Socialist organisation.

THE ASSURANCE OF THE WORKING MAN.

Labour in these days is becoming quite respectable and is patronized in such exalted quarters that even Fan extract from the lordly " Times " newspaper becomes quite appropriate in this column. The subject matter of the extract is a certain insur- ance company, which shall be nameless, as I am quite I sure the Editor of the " Examiner " does not believe in free advertisements. The extract reads as follows: 1—" The history of this concern was very remarkable as it showed what vast sums were drawn from the pockets of those who were the least able to bear them. It had been said that the question with the working classes was not so much, how to invest their savings as how to get money to save, but the figures about to be quoted would show that the working classes were I deeply interested in the question as to what would be the best mode of investing their savings. About 60 years ago the Company started with £2,500 capital. A few years ago the nominal capital was increased to £2oo,oooby bonuses paid to shareholders re-invested in new shares, it being said that it looked better to pay 5 per cent. on £200,000 nominal capital than 100 per cent. on £lO,OOO nominal capital." Thus far the "Times." The moral follows later,but one may pause a while to interjeet the query: Is it likely capitalists will leave the country where the Funearned increment multiplied their capital a hundred fold and if they did leave would not their exodus be followed by a deuteronomy and a leviticus of laws against their return? The superior jove-like " Man- chester Guardian" also is of service to us in this instance for it quotes Sir Herbert Maxwell as follows : "It may be interesting to menticm some facts in the history of the largest and most powerful of these institutions (Insurance) in order to show you what immense fortunes the working men of this country are willing to allow clever persons to make out of their savings—by perfectly fair means, understand. (Yes, Sir Herbert, we are beginning to under- stand.) The Company was started a little more than 60 years ago with a capital of £lO,OOO, of which £2,500 was paid up, but the profits grew steadily till they amounted to about 100 times the nominal capital, when the capital was raised by Act of Parliament to £200,000, the property of a small body of share- holders. In addition £500,000 as divided amongst the shareholders as bonuses in 1891. , It would appear from this statement that a share- holder contributing £25 to the original capital of the Company would receive no less a sum than £5,100 in dividends and bonuses in 1891." The readers at the " Voice " will now better understand what is meant by the term, "the Law 4nd the Profits." The yearly income of this Company from industrial classes of insurance cost the Company about 40 per cent. in expenses and commission, so that out of every is. paid by a workman to make some little temporary: provision for his wife and family, nearly sd. goes in expenses. Yet the Company pays claims promptly and makes a fine handsome profit for the shareholders. The collectors and rank and file don't seem to be millionaires.

The original £2,500 capital designed to meet claims is an infinitesimal safeguard in view of the immense number of policies, and moreover the capital has been repaid to the shareholders a thousand times over. The guarantee of payment of claims when due is not the capital of the Company but the accumulated funds, which consists of premiums paid by the work- ing classes, and interest on same earned by the working classes. Those funds are an exampje of what can be done, even under a competitive system, by cl-oreration indirectly exercised for mutual safeguard. After repaying capital many times over, after paying generous expenses. of management and rent of ability, after paying claims presented there remains a sum of ,£23,000.000 as security against future needs, in one industrial assurance company alone. Those funds are a monument to the thrift, self-denial, patience, and stupidity, of the working classes of this realm. Insurance companies fill an important office in human economy and I say nothing against a scheme so necessary under present conditions; but I do want the yworking class to have a little more assurance so that they may realise their power and importance even in financial matters. And perhaps, who knows, it may eventually dawn upon them that it. would be as well to secure the profits by obtaining co-operative owner- ship and collective control of funds they themselves provide. Space forbids more just now, but like my namesake I will return from temporary retirement in due time, and revive the subject. LAZARUS. TRADES UNIONS AND SOCIALISM.

Sir, —"Socialist Tkades Unionist" scoffs at my remarks about him being a young man in a hurry, but admits one very important fact, viz., that Socialists have in the past and will more in the future attack the political beliefs of the trade unionists, which is tantamount to saying that if the Socialists can have their own way the trades unions will cease to be trades unions in the strict and old sense of the word, and become societies of a purely particular character which must of a necessity be Socialistic. • ' Now, Sir,your correspondent says I must be lacking in my knowledge of Trades Unionism simply, I pre- sume, because I uphold men who have been for • a great number of years and are to-day prominent ia all matters affecting the welfare of the organised workers; but compare these men's Works with some of the hair brained schemes of tbo Socialists. One of them had the audacity at the last meeting of the Warrington Town Council to propose that the Corporation should buy and sell coal and so save (?) a sum of 4s. per ton. Well, sir, it is the first time, that I have ever heard of a Town Council which could sell anything cheaper than private enterprise can, unless they hold a monopoly or otherwise subsidise it out of the rates.

MY DEAR

Very many thanks to you all for your kind letters to me. I think -it very good indeed of you to trouble to write when you want perhaps to be out and about in the sunshine. Perhaps some of you did not see the announce- ment of the new Competition in last week's paper. I thought that many of you would he able to send me a short account of your holiday, so this Competition starts on August 20th, and will be open for a fortnight. Six nice boxes of Fry's Chocolate will be awarded for the six best accounts received. Mirrors are sent this week to Nellie Been and Evelyn France. Don't forget the picture post- cards of your holiday haunts. Yours, with love, UNCLE NED.

--#_- ME AND KITTY.

We're twins, me and Kitty. I'm the other one—Tommy. I'm five years old to-morrow, and Kitty's just as old as me, only Kitty's a girl.

- To-morrow's my birthday and Kitty's too; we've both got the same one. We always get presents, mamma says. I don't remenarier, for I haven't had a great many birthdays, or Kitty hasn't, either. Me and Kitty wanted to give presents to us, too. I mean Kitty wanted to give me a present and I wanted to give Kitty a present. We asked mamma to give me half-a-sovereign and Kitty half-a-sovereign. She said she thought she'd rather give me half-a-crown and Kitty half-a- crown. She showed us the half-a-crown, and we said yes.elt's lots of •money. I know it's more than half-a-sovereign. Mamma let us go down town. I mean she let me take Kitty down town, for Kitty's a girl, and I always take care of her. Mamma said we ought not to go together, for then I'd know what Kitty bought, and Kitty'd know what I bought. But we said we wouldn't look, for we wanted to be s'prised. We never had such a pile of money before. I said to Kitty what did she think was a nice present/ and she said, Oh, a big doll, with really hair and sure-enough eyes, and a pink dress and button boots and things. And I said I thought the nicest thing was a great big horse that would go. When we came to the store I told Kitty not to look, and I went to buy a. doll. There was a splendid one, I tell you, and I told the man to put a piece of paper on it. Then I gave him all my money, and told him I wanted a little of it back to buy candy and nuts and things. But he said the doll would cost more than all my half-crown. Then I told him I'd buy a set of doll's china dishes, but he said they would cost more, too. _

They had a lot of peanuts there. I like peanuts, 190 I told him I'd buy some peanuts for my half-crown. It was an awful lot pf peanuts. Kitty likes peanuts, too. Then I went where Kitty was. She had two things with paper on. I told her I wouldn't know what they were for anything; it's only girls that want to know everything, but I wanted to feel which they IA ere, hard or soft. But Kitty was real mean, and wouldn't let me. Then I asked her were they red or blue, or green or white, and she wouldn't tell me.

But when we were going home Kitty said she'd show me, the least little bit. She did. It was streaky red. It was candy. Then. I said wouldn't she give me a bite? And she said yes, if I'd take just a little bite. And she held the paper up a little and put her finger where I was to bite, just so far. Then I took a bite, but I guess I tried to bite bigger. I guess I bit Kitty's finger, for she jumped, and I was leaning over to get a, big bite, and somehow I tumbled over, and you ought to have seen. the peanuts spill! There were peanuts in the street, and peanuts in the gutter, and peanuts all over the side-walk. And when I tumbled I tumbled over Kitty, and she tumbled over, too. And the streaky red thing was broke to smash, and it was a candy cane. And the other thing went out in the street, and a baker's cart was coining, and it ran over the thing, and Kitty cried. Girls always cry. Then the baker's boy he picked it uP, and he was sorry, and he said it was good yet, and he'd fix it, and he gave me a big cake and Kitty a big cake. It was a tin horse, and it laad wheels and a boy and a tail on it, and it wasn't good yet, for the tail was off and the wheels wobbled.

Me and Kitty ate up the candy cane. Then we picked up some of the peanuts, but we got awfully tired, and we sat down on some steps and ate some peanuts. Then we ate up the cakes. Then we ate some more peanuts. Then there were only a few left, and we ate up all the peanuts. Then we took the string the man tied round the peanuts and/ tied it on the tin horse to draw him home.

N. DOYLE

The centre of gravity: the lowest button on a Quaker's vest.

A teacher asked: "What bird is large enough to carry off a man ?" Nobody knew; but one little girl suggested "a lark." And then she ex- plained: "Mamma said papa wouldn't be home until Saturday, because he had gone off on a lark."

"Waiter, this bit of turbot is not so good as that you gave us last Sunday." Waiter: "Beg pardon, sir; it's off the very same fish."

"In the sentence, 'John strikes William,'' remarked a school-teacher, "what is the object of strikes?" "Higher wages and less work," promptly replied the intelligent youth.
