PURVEYORS EY SPECIAL APPOINTMENT TO 11.1. M. THE EMPRESS OF RUSSIA

- Poor Mollie was an orphan, lane-C • o earn enough money to buy food, and to pay for her night's lodging, she went out every day to sell flowers in the London streets, Her bright smile and cheerful voice as she addressed the passers by attracted a great many people to buy her flowers, but even then Molly found it difficult to make the small sum she needed for her daily food and shelter. But there was one day in the week when she always felt sure of emptying her basket, and, often, of having an extra penny or two. That was the visiting day at the great hospital near which she usually stood, when many of the visitors were induced to take a bunch of fresh, sweet-scented flowers to the invalids they were going to see. It was on one of these visiting days that a small boy, looking very forlorn and neglected, stopped before Mollie's basket of roses and gazed at them wist- fully. Then he held out a halfpenny very timidly. "Please, will you give me a ha'porth of them for mother in the hospital?" he said, The girl who stood next to Mollie laughed rather scornfully. "Why, kiddie, those roses cost twopence apiece in the market!" The boy's face fell, and he was about to move on, when Molly stoppel him. "Here you are," she said, with a bright smile, as she thrust into his hand a bunch of the sweet, red roses. "There, run along with them to mother," she said, and the astonished child obeyed very happily. "Whatever did you de that for?" asked Mollie's companion. "You could have sold them for at least a shilling!" But Mollie only laughed cheerfully, as she re-arranged the rest of her flowers. I think, though, that One who sees every kind action would say: "Inas- much as ye have done it unto the least of these.- EVELYN FRANCE. THE CHATTERBOX. the sight of this young maiden. hall I tell you why All wise folk who chance to meet her Hastily would fly? Chitter-chathr, pitter-patter, Like the pelting rain; When you think that she has finished Off she goes again. All day long her tongue is wagging, And if you peep In her room at night you would find her, Talking in her sleep. Every thought of passing fancy Flitting through her brain, Out it comes like pouring wafer, Nothing can restrain. Should you have some news to publish, There's one simple way, • Tell her—all the house will know it In a single day. True or false, or wise or foolish, Tale of joy or woe, Sets her tongue in ceaseless action— Always on the go. If there is no human being ' Near to hear her chat, She will talk to any creature— Bird, or deg, or cat. In the Book where wisest counsels We are bid to seek, We learn there is a time for silence, And a time to speak. If the maid had only folowed What the wise man said, Then no more, to escape her clatter, All her friends had fled. Cackling hens and gobbling turkeys, Geese, and crowing cocks, Scarcely mike a greater hub-bub- Than this Chatterbox. Ghitter-chatter, pitter-patter, Like the pelting rain; When you trust that she has finished, Off she goes again! REGINALD 'WARBURTON. GIRLS' NAMES.

Mary, the commonest of all names given to woman, is yet one of the sweetest. Byron assures us that he had an absolute passion for it. It signifies " exalted "; Maria and Marie— the latter French—are only other forms for Mary, and have the same meaning. Martha signifies "bitterness." Anne, Anna, Hannah, and probably Nancy, are from the same • source, and signify "kind," or "gracious." Ellen was originally Helen, the Lai in being Helena, and the French Helene—the meaning being, accord- ing to some, "alluring," and according to others, "one who pities.", Jane signifies the same as Sarah, or Sally, "a princess, and the morning star." Susan, "a lily"; Rebecca, " plump "; Lucy, "brightness of aspect"; Louisa or Louise, "one who protects"; Frances, or Fanny, "frank or free"; Catharine, "pure or chaste"; Caroline and Charlotte, "queens"; Sophia, " wisdom "; Emma, " tender "; Mar- garet, "a pearl or a daisy"; Elizabeth and Eliza, " true "; Julia, Juliette, and Julietta, " soft-haired "; Agnes, " chaste " ;, Amelia and Amy, " beloved "; Clara, "clear or bright"; Eleanor, "all faithful "; Gertrude, "all truth"; Graze, " favour " ; Laura, "a laurel "; Matilda, "a noble or brave maid"; Phoebe, "light of life"; Amanda, " amiable "; Isabel, "true and lovely"; Pauline, "little one"; Olive, "peace"; Edith, "happy."

If you desire to enjoy life, avoid unpunctual persons; they impede business and, poison pleature. Make it your own rule not only to be punctual, but a little beforehand. Such a habit secures a composure which is essential to happiness. For want of it many persons live in a constant fever, and put all about them in a fever too.

A Jeisey man lost an axe more than twenty years ago, which he has just found under his bed. His life hasn't been made unhappy by house-cleaning.

A minister once asked a condemned criminal in a Paris jail, "What kind of a conscience have you?" "It's as good as new," re.plied the prisoner, "for I have never used it."

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

A VANISHING LAKE.

Fresh evidence of . the disappearance of Lake Tchad in Central Africa has been ac- cumulated. Captain Tilho, of the Anglo- French Boundary Commission, says that the form and area of the lake have undergone great changes since the explorations of Barth and Nlphtigal, forty or fifty years ago. It is now navigable only in certain places, and boa t's frequently run aground. The tendency seems to be towards the development of a vast marsh on the site of the lake, although hall-a-century ago its great waves, in stormy weather gave it the appearance of an ocean.

===== 0002601_19070824_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPER LIGHTNING-PROTECTORS.

In an. address to the Royal Engineers at Chatham, Mr. Alfred Hands said that too much importance is attached to the form and composition of lightning-conductors, and not enough to the fact that the efficiency depends almost entirely on the way the apparatus is attached, and little on what it is made of. Lightning-conductors in the hands of ex- perts, tie said, may be likened to drugs in the hands of doctors—they must be suited to the particular case. As to the relative value of iron and copper for conductors; there is but a trifling difference, except that for con- ductors expected to last long iron is too perishable. •

===== 0002601_19070824_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
FORTY-ONE INCHES OF RAIN.

The most remarkable rainfall on record seems to have been that of a thunderstorm in Suva, Fiji, on the night of August Bth, 1906. Mr. R. L. Holmes reports to the Royal Meteorological Society of London that the fall was continuous from six p.m. until sunrise of the following day. and that the rain - gauge holding 12•5 in. was over- flowing at ten p.m., again at two a.m., and a third time at six a.m. This is an actual measurement of more than 37in. Besides the unknown overflow, it is supposed that there was a further loss of about 11 per cent. on account of the height of the gauge, which was sft. above the ground. The conclusion is that not less than 41in. of rain must have fallen in about thirteen hours.

===== 0002601_19070824_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MIDGET EARTH.

We regard the earth as a fairly large affair; yet it is but a midget ball in com- parison with the sun. The volume of the sun, astronomers would have us believe, Is 1,250,000 times greater than that of the earth. Perhaps it is some consolation to learn that its weight is not in equal ratio. In mass the sun is only equal to about 315,000 earths. At the same time it is rather stagger- ing to learn that the mass of the sun is equal to 750 times the united masses of all the bodies which gravitate round it and receive its light and heat. What it lack § in density it makes up for in size, so that it has no difficulty in attracting the planets and keep- ing them whirling around itself at their re- spective distances.

===== 0002601_19070824_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN THE EARTH WILL BE PULL.

Professor ,Ravenstein, of the Royal Geo- graphical Society, has estimated that the fer- tile lands of the globe amount to 28,000,000 square miles, the steppes to 14,000,000, and the deserts to A,000,000. Fixing 207 persons to the square mile for fertile lands, ten for steppes, and one for deserts as the greatest population that the earth could properly nourish, the Professor arrives at the conclu- sion that when the number of inhabitants reaches about 6,000,000,000, the earth will be peopled to its full capacity. At present it contains somewhat more than one-quarter of that number. If the rate of increase shewn by the latest census statistics should be uni- formly maintained, Professor Ravenstein (thews that the globe would be fully peopled about the year 2072—about a century and a- half hence.

===== 0002601_19070824_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
A MAN-MADE NIAGARA.

The great cataract in the New River, formed in the Imperial Valley, California, by the escape of the waters of the Colorado liiver, has been likened to Niagara Falls. It varies from 90ft. to 100 ft. in height, and is from 1,500 to 1,800 yards broad. It likewise. resembles Niagara in eating backwards, or upstream, but its progress in this direction is extremely rapid, amounting to about one- third of a mile per day. This arises from thc fact that the channel of the stream is cut through the fragile material deposited cen- turies ago by the Colorado River at the head of the Gulf of California. It is predicted that if the escape of the waters of the Colorado is not arrested before the cataract Ills cut back far enough to unite the New ll.ver and the Alamo River, the Imperial Val- ley will be entirely deprived of its irrigatio streams. This cataract may be called "man- made," since its existence is due to his in- terference with the waters of the Colorado.

===== 0002601_19070824_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HEAT OF THE SUN.

The radiation of the solar heat is modified by the earth's atmosphere. If allowance is made for the heat absorbed by the atmo- sphere, Blame idea can be formed of what that heat really is. The amount of it nor- mally received on a square metre (something more than a yard) of the earth would suffice to boil a quart of water in less than six minutes. Supposing that square metre of earth were advanced to the actual surface of the sun, the radiation received there per second, if converted into force, would repre- sent toe power of 77,000 horses. What is the total amount of heat radiated by the sun? It is equal to that which would be produced by burning upon every square metre of its sur- face at least six and a-half tons of coal per hour, and keeping up that rate of consump- tion constantly; for the sun's heat has not diminished in millions of years. The sun pours forth , its light and heat in every direction; and so exorbitantly prodigal is his majesty that the whole of the planets, great and small, receive only the .227-millionth part of the whole quantity of light and heat dispersed. The rest is lost in interstellar space. The share that our earth receives is less than the 2,000-millionth part of the whole supply.

===== 0002601_19070824_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PHENOMENA OF THE MOON.

In "The ;Nloon in Modern Astronomy," Mr. Philip Fauth sets forth it new view of. the . moon's constitution. In the first

place the author dwells •on the difficulty of interpreting the appearances of the moon's surface in terms of terrestrial volcanology. The so-called volcanoes of the moon cannot, he thinks, be due to the causes which have produced those of the earth. Their enor- mous relative size, for example, is difficult to explain, on the view that they are the result of similar forces. One of the lunar features, known to astronomers as Clavius, has a diameter of 142 miles, and is enclosed by walls three miles high. When this is com- pared with the average size of the terrestrial volcano, and when it is remembered that the earth is fifty times as large as the moon, the difficulty of accounting for lunar "vol- canoes " by forces similar to those which have produced the volcanoes of the earth is axmarnnt. And that the moon. although So much smaller than the eartn, snouid have mountain peaks rising to greater heights than those of the Himalayas, is another difficulty.

To a-mount, then, for the known pheno- mena of the moon, Mr. Fauth proposes

THE FOLLOWING LUNAR THEORY: Our satellite must be covered with a thick layer of ice, for nothing else, he -declares, will account for the appearances he has ob- served during the fourteen-days' lunar day. Beneath this crust of ice is an ocean which by its motions—due to tides, &c.—has pro- duced the scarred and fractured surface with which we are familiar. Inside these shells of ice and water lies a solid nucleus which may be looked upon as the real moon. The author of this new theory agrees with the older view that the moon has no atmo- sphere. But it is difficult to understand how it could have a surface of solid water with- out a conseauent envelone of water vapour.. Evaporation would take place trom me sur- face of the ice forming an aqueous atmo- sphere, from which the moisture would be continually returned by precipitation as snow.

A DROWNING MYSTERY.

Mr. S. Brighouse conducted an inquiry at the Warring- ton Police Court on Monday concerning the death of Alfred Hanson (28), 128, Evelyn-street, ironworker, whose body was found in the Sankey and St. Helens Canal on Thursday the 15th inst. at 7.45 p.m. Joseph Hanson said the deceased was his son. In answer to the Coroner witness said his son was perfectly sober.

David Roscow said at eight o'clock last Monday night he called at the Sloop Hotel, Sankey Bridges, where he saw deceased. Hanson had a glass of beer. They afterwards went for a walk round and called at the Hose Inn, Hood-lane. There were five of them and they as well as Hanson had a share of four quarts of beer. Deceased was not drunk. They went for another walk round, but, on arriving at Buttermilk Bridge the deceased stayed behind, his friends walking on. Hanson was not. drunk.

Patrick Quinn, 10, Mount-street said he was in the Rose Inn along with deceased. They had four quarts ot beer between five of them.

Robert Kirk said at 7.30 on Thursday night he went with the grappling irons to the Sankey and St. Helens canal and commenced grappling operations. Questioned by the coroner Kirk said the reason why be did not commence operations on Tuesday or Wednesday was because he was not sure whether Hanson was drowned or not. He afterwards found the body. On examining it he found no marks of violence or any signs of foul play. The Coroner said a share of four quarts of beer was not much to an ironworker. There was no doubt that the deceased was sober. __

The jury returned a verdict of "Found Drowned there being no evidence to show how the body got into the water.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

EDUCATION AS A NATIONAL CHARGE.

A meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday under the presi- dency of Mr. Joseph Spruce. There were also present: —Messrs. J. W. Temperley, S. Woodward, Thomas Wright, C. E. Linaker, Thomas Guest, J. Hallows, G. Egerton-Warburton, Admiral Jones-Parry, A. Broome, C. F. Bell, Charles Roylance, J. Mynekyme, L. Bolton, R. Nickson, J. H. Davies, E. Britch, George Piling, J. Douglas, Eaton Bretherton, W. Weir, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. R. de Prez, T.Cook, and J. Pearson (relieving officers.)

===== 0002601_19070824_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKHOUSE ALTERA.TIONS.

The Local Government Board transmitted an order authorising the Guardians to make alterations and additions to the Workhouse at a cost not exceeding £2,395 and to borrow that sum, the loan to be repaid within a period not exceeding 27 years. THE NEW BUILDINGS.

Messrs. W. and S. Owen wrote stating that the new buildings, comprising nurses' home and children's and maternity block, were sufficiently advanced for the Board to enter into occupation of them on August 21st. A committee was adopted to deal with the furnish- ing of the premises. THE TREATMENT OF IMBECILES. rho Conway Union requested the Runcorn Guardians to pass a resolution urging the local Government Board to bring some measure before the Goverment with the object of establishing homes for imbeciles with a view to relieving the present undue pressure on the accom- modation of lunatic asylums and workhouses, believing that such persons could be more adequately cared for and trained to do work which would be advantageous to the institutions as well as to the inmates. The Board decided to support the proposal. COST OF EDI:CA.TION. The Uttoxeter Union forwarded for approval and support, a resolution protesting strongly against the ever-increasing education rate, which they considered a most unfair burden on the land and other rateable hereditaments in meal districts, and should be borne out of the Imperial Exchequer. Mr. Linaker said he was thoroughly in sympathy with the object of the communication. Every county was suffering at the present moment from extravagance in the administration of the 1902 Act. As education had become a compulsory matter, ha thought it was time a move was made to put taxation for educational purposes on a broader basis. Education should be a national charge administered by local authorities. He moved that the resolution be supported. Mr. Douglas seconded. Mr. Woodward thought it would be a grave mistake to put the whole cost on the national exchequer and would tend in some cases to great irregularities. He was strongly of opinion that they ought not to support the letter. Mr. Linaker said that if all the cost were paid out of the imperial exchequer, there would be a superior body which would restrain the County Education Committees from spending as much money as they now expended. Mr. Woodward: lam afraid that is doubtful. Mr. Hallows: They could not spend more than they do now. Mr. Linaker: So far as I know, at present there is no control over County Councils as to what they spend. It is quite time there was a controlling hand over them. The Board agreed to support the resolution. THANKS. The House Committee resolved that the best thanks of the Guardians be conveyed to Mrs. Blair,of the Elms, Moore, for the treat to hospital patients, old women and children at Moore on the Bth mat; and to Mrs. Jones Parry, of Thelwall Hall, for her kindness in pro- viding eight weeks' holiday for Ada Percival. FINANCIAL.

Mr. Woodward reported payments in out relief as follows :—Mr. Cook £75 ; Mr. de Prez £3O; Mr. Pearson £25. The total payments amounted to £l4l 4s. The balance in favour of the Board was £4,332 Os. 9d. On the architects' certificate the Board paid on account to Mr. Fairclough, in connection with the new build- ings, £lBO.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Gough —Hutchings.

The picturesquely situated church of St. Laurence, Frodsham, was filled with a fashionable congregation on Wednesday afternoon on the occasion of the marriage of Mr. Cyril Philip Gough, youngest son of the late Mr. Philip H. Gough, of Wem, Shropshire, and Miss Winifrid Mary Hutchings, eldest daughter of Mr. William Nelson Hutchings, J.P., of Hilsboro', Frodsham, a partner in the firm of Messrs. J. and W. N. Hutchings, tanners, Warrington. The wedding aroused great interest, and there were numerous visitors from Warrington, where the bride and bride- groom are well known and esteemed, Mr. Gough being the secretary of the Warrington Musical Society. The church was beautifully decorated with marguerites, lilies, and other white flowers, the work reflecting considerable credit on Mrs. Blogg, Miss Violet Hayes, I and other friends. The officiating clergy were the I Rev. H. B. Blogg (vicar), and the Rev. F. B. Corfield (curate). The service was fully choral. Mr. C. H. I Hibbertt presided at the organ and played the pro- ; cessional music from " Lohengrin," the Wedding March from "Lohengrin," "Reverie " (Hibbertt)," Salut ; d'Amour " (E tgar) "March in G-" (Smart) and ; Mendelssohn's " Wedding Mech." The hymns sung were" Now thank we all our God," "0 perfect love," "Thine for ever." The bride, who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired in an ivory i duchess° satin bridal gown. The bodice was made with draped corsage and fichu, and ornamented with rich hand-worked lace, the gored skirt falling full ' from Empire back, with folds of the satin round the foot. The gown, which was of charming simplicity, I was completed by a full Court train of satin, festooned I and decorated with orange blossom, chiffon frilling, and tiny "good luck " shoe. She carried a shower bouquet of white roses, lilies of the valley, orange blossom, and white heather. She w- re a Brussels net veil edged with beautiful Honiton lace, lent by her grandmother, and used by her at her wedding, as well as at the wedding of the bride's mother. The bride's ornaments were a pearl and olavine ring, the gift of the bridegroom, and a dainty horseshoe of white heather, the gift of Mrs. Blogg. The bridesmaids were the Misses Dorothea Hutchings and Rosalind Hutchings (sisters), and Lilian Petrie and EdithH Leake, and the train-bearers the Misses Dorothy Scott (cousin of the bride) and Molly Brnndrit. The bridesmaids wore dresses of ivory crepe de chine over shaded shell pink silk foundations, and bodices made in fashionable Kimona style, with delicate sky- coloured chiffon taffeta bands, and finished with soft bows of the same ; also transparent lace yoke with touches of silver tissue, skirts full with Empire backs, finished at foot with three tucks and bands of blue. The charming effect of coloured foundations and silk trimmings attracted much admiration. They also wore ivory crinoline and lace brim hats, with full chiffon taffeta crowns embroidered with pink roses, and large pale blue bows in front to match the blue of the dresses. Their bouquets consisted of Viscountess of Folkestale roses tied with blue ribbon, and they wore gold and opal safety pin brooches, the gifts of the bridegroom. The train-bearets' dresses were made of the same materials and colouring as the bridesmaids' gowns, in pinafore shape, with soft yoke and sleeves of Brussels net and lace. Their hats were made entirely of narrow Valenciennes lace, trimmed with a big bow of pale blue in front, and tied with long blue strings. They wore initial safety pin brooches, set with pearls, also the gifts of the bride- groom. The mother of the bride wore a beautiful gown of ivy green taffeta made in draped Kimona style, with clever introductions of deep cream-coloured embroidery lace insertions, a front of Brussels lace, relieved by a panel of self-coloured silk brought to a point. The skirt fell full, and was effectively finished with bands of the silk, edged with velvet round the foot. The velvet both on skirt and bodice put the finishing touch to the dress. Her hat was made of cream panne, bound with ivy green velvet trimmed with a cluster of Gloire de Dijon roses, out of which swept a beauifully shaded ostrich plume. Her bouquet was composed of Gloire de Dijon roses. Mr. Arthur R. C. Gough (brother of the bride- groom) acted as best man.

At the conclusion of the ceremony a reception was held at Hillsboro' and about 200 guests attended. Later in the afternoon, with the heartiest congratula- tions and the best wishes of their friends, the happy cuiple drove to Warrington, to take the train for York en route for Northumberland, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was of pastel blue cloth with touches of mauve panne velvet, and deep belt of the same. The deep collar was embroidered with palest pink and aluminium braid.lier hat consisted of pastel blue chiffon taffeta, lined with rows of narrow Valenciennes lace, and with full crown trimmed simply by a wreath of pansies. the knee to Pendleton Belmont. "Dead Length" in the" Daily Dispatch " has the following notes oil the match :

The final for the Lancashire and Cheshire Club Championship was inthued with more than usual interest seeing that the invincible champions for tha last four years—Leigh Railway—had been defeated in the semi- final by Warrington Bull's Head, who were opposed by Pendleton Belmont. The latter had achieved a note- worthy triumph over Newton-le-Willows in the other semi-final, and as the West Lancashire team had lifted the Liverpool and District cup from Rainhill, they had great hopes of being hailed amateur champions for the first time.

Pendleton won, and won easily, and though the men from Warrington zomplained that the Northumberland green was unlit for such a vital con- test, I am sure the allegation WAS only made in a moment following a bitter defeat. The sward, presided over by Mr. W. E. W. Ashworth, could not have been in better condition. It is, as every practical player knows, a" late " green,as far as condition is concerned, and if, as several of the Warrington men stated, it was far too easy, then why did they bowl so badly on it? Most of the Leigh railwaymen were present, and were astonished at their conquerers in the semi-final,

‘. Why," said one," how on earth they beat us on this form passes comprehension." A suggestion from the writer that Pendleton were exhibiting wonderful form met with grunts and half-heard threats of what" we would do with Belmont if only we had been playing them." Certainly the local team thoroughly deserved the championship, which practically carries with it the highest honours in crown green bowling.

As a matter of fact, the Warrington men held too great a faith in the exploded "round peg "myth. On their own little and tricky green this method may well confound and. upset the stranger, but on a neutral sward the nearest way to the Jack pays best, and hence it was that only three of the ex-champions proved successful. I do not propose to dwell upon the dry details of each game. It would be out of place to do so, unless to gratify individual satisfaction, but I must congratulate the Pendleton Club on bringing the championship to Manchester for the first time since Didsbury won in 1902.

The winning side only lost three games, and if Llewellyn and that member of a notorious football family, Albert Valentine, lost rather heavily, they had the satisfaction of knowing that they gained sufficient points to make the championship safe when Robert Nfaxwell and Pairbrother fought out the last and keenest game of the day. They are one and all plucky and sportmanlike players, and won the championship in the absence of such sound players as Albert Pendlebury and Charles Hough, both of whom were too ill to take part in one of the most memorable finals on record. The majority of 20 in favour of Pendleton and the con- sequent custody of the cup at the Eccles Old-road club will do the game a dell of good, if only to prove that the all-powerful West Lancashire clubs are not invul- nerable.

The magnificent championship cup, presented by Mr. Oros&ld, M.P. for Warrington, the president of the association, who also gives gold medals to the winning team and gold-centre medals to the losers, was handed over to the winners by ND.. J. W. G. Coombs, of Olds- bury, the depTity president and chairman of the association, who had acted as referee. The cup was received by Mr. R. Tomlinson, Pendleton's captain.

Houghton—Owen.

St. Mary's Church, Sankey, was the scene of a pretty wedding on Tuesday, when Mr. Robert William Houghton, eldest son of the late Mr. William Houghton, and of Mrs. Houghton, Hal! Nook, Penketh, and grandson of Mr. W. D. Houghton, was married to Miss Annie Owen, second daughter of Mr. Thomas Owen of Vicars Green Farm, Great Sankey. Although the weather was very unfavour- able, there was a large congregation. The Rev. J. R. Jones officiated. The bride, who was given away by her father, wore a travelling costume of slate grey colour, a white felt hat adorned with an ostrich plume, and a feather boa. There was only one bridesmaid— Miss Annie Allanby, of Grange-over-Sands (cousin), who was attired in cream, with a cream crinoline hat to match. There were no bouquets, the bride carrying a Prayer-book. After a reception at Vicar's Green Farm, the bride and bridegroom departed for Aberyst- wyth to spend the honeymoon. They have received a large number of presents.

White—Millington.

A wedding which aroused considerable local interest was solemnized at the Parish Church on Wednesday, when Mr. Frank Eaglesfield White, son of Mr. James White, of Manchester-road, was married to Miss Emily Theresa Millington, of liattersby-lane. The Rev. T. A. J. Ricipath officiated, while Mr. George White (brother of the bridegroom) acted as best man. The bride, who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired in white silk, with a mauve bat. She was attended by two bridesmaids, Miss Catherine E. White and Miss Florence Millington, who looked exceedingly pretty in their pale blue costumes. Mr. Joseph Gorst played appropriate selections on the organ, including Mendelssohn's " Wedding March." After the ceremony a reception was held at the house of the bride's father, a large number of guests being present. Later on the happy couple left for North Wales, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was of blue serge. A large number of handsome and valuable presents have been received.

Bouchier—Richardson.

There was an exceedingly pretty wedding at St. Paul's Church on Wednesday when Captain Charles Fitzhemy Bouchier, of the 2nd V.B. Loyal North Lancashire Regiment, was married to Miss Ethel Richardson, daughter of Mr. James Richardson, Palmyra House, Springfield-street. Considerable interest was taken in the happy event and a large congregation assembled. The officiating clergy were the Rev. W. Bracecamp (vicar) and the Rev. E. E. Peters (curate). Wilkes presided at the Organ and played festal music including Mendelssohn's "Wedding March." She carried a lovely bouquet of white roses and white heather. Water Polo.

It was not with any confidence that Warrington entered upon their match with Wigan on Thursday evening, for twice previously this season had the colliers inflicted defeat upon them in no half-hearted fashion. There was a large crowd to witness the encounter at the Warrington Baths and considerable excitement prevailed. Warrington were again unable to withstand the vigorous attack of the colliers and were beateu by eight goals to two. The teams were as follows :—Warrington: S. Walker, goal; W. Sutton and W. Ashton, backs ; C. Ashton, R.Allen,T.Pemberton and C. Turner, forwards. Wigan: C. S. Smith, goal ; W. M`Grath and G. W. Nevison, backs ; T. Saunders, J. Martin, I. Bentham, and J. Critchley, forwards.

At the outset Wigan defended the deep end. They quickly assumed the aggressive, and several shots narrowly missed the goal. Play became exciting and two players were ordered out. Bentham opened the scoring for Wigan, and a few minutes later Critchley added a second. C. Ashton displayed splendid form, and registered two goals, and half-time arrived with the scores equal. On resuming, Martin gave Wigan the lead again, and they maintained the upper hand to the close, goals being added by Martin, Bentham (3), and Saunders (2).

Towards the close the proceedings were distinctly lively, the decisions of the referee being frequently resented by the Warrington spectators who gave expres- sion to their feelings by "booing "vigorously.

Great interest was taken in the semi-final for the Warrington Works Squadron Competition which was decided prior to the match. Co-operative Stores beat Rylands's, and Pearson and Knowles were victorioos over the Corporation. -

HATTON SHOW. Villagers' Creditable Display.

Hatton is one of those charming, old-world villagers whose inhabitants take a delight in making their gardens beautiful. Naturally they are not a little proud of the remits of their handiwork and the annual show promoted by the Horticultural Society is looked forward to as the leading event of the year. Notwithstanding the bad season, this year's exhibition which was held on Thursday in a field at Hatton Hall, which had been placed at the disposal of the society by Mr. James Parr, was highly creditable. The entries constituted a record and were 100 in advance of last year, totalling 670. The quality of the exhibits generally was good, and this applies in particular to the bush fruit. There was a splendid collection of flowers, and the table decorations were exquisite. Two magnifi- cent groups of plants (not for competition) lent by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart. and Mr. T. H. Lyon attracted general admiration. Mrs. Bart., Acton Grange had a good display of fruit. The cottage gardens which had been judged were a credit to the district, the judges being surprised to find them in such fine condition, considering the unfavourable weather. The following discharged the duties of judges :—Farmers' Produce: Messrs. Samuel Davies and William Hough. Cottagers' gardens and produce : Messrs. William Turner and D. Mcßride. Honey: Mr. Herbert Potts. Butter and eggs : Miss Collins. Table decorations : Mrs. Wynne- Jones and Mrs. J. J. Whitley. Baskets of wild flowers : Mrs. Seeker and Miss Litton. The arrangements for the show were all that could be desired, and special praise is due to 'qr. J. G. Hatton for the efficient way in which he discharged the secretarial duties.

There was a large attendance at the opening of the show by Mrs. Ussher, of the Dene, Great Budworth. The President of the Society tßev. the Hon. C. F. Cross) presided. Mrs. Ussher expressed the pleasure it gave her to he amongst them on such a pleasant occasion. She hoped it was the beginning of a long period of friendship between them. The culture of flowers was a very pleasant hobby and brought out the best in human character. She Vtr:l3 very pleased to know that the exhibits this year showed the record number of 670- 100 more than last year—and that there was such a large entry from the local cottagers. That must be a matter of congratulation to the members of the Society. In these times of unrest and rapid movement and making of money, it was a pleasure to be able to seek repose in thd gardens where they could watch the opening of the flowers in all their beauty. Flower culture was the most refining of human occupations and she was glad it had taken such a hold of the people of Hatton. She would look forward to future shows held in such delightful surroundings. (Applause.). A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mrs. Ussher on the proposition of Mr. J. Wright seconded by Mr. Warburton.

Mr. Parr was thanlcAd for lending the field, on the motion of Mr. T. H. West seconded by Mr. T. J. Lee. Mr. J. E. Seolcer moved and Mr. Hatton seconded a similar compliment to the Chairman. _

The Rev. he Hon. C. 11., Cross in reeponding said the society was doing a valuable work in H..tton awl the committee were to be heartily congratulated on the excellent show. He desired particularly to thank Mr. J. G. Hatton for his energetic work as secreraty and the iniges for the conscientious way in which they had performed their duties. During the afternoon the Walton Band played selec- tions and an interesting programme of sports was gone through.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football. Have you got one of our "Football Annuals " yet ? It is the best pennyworth of facts, fixtures and photis published, and moreover it is a local annual. The contents are of interest to all local sports enthusiasts. isk your newsagents for a copy.

People are beginning already to talk and think of football, and discuss the prospects of their favourite teams. There is considerable interest manifested in Warrington in the forthcoming season, for it is recognised that the Cup-holders will have an arduous campaign. Their possession of the honour is an added responsibility which will make itself felt, for every team will be doubly anxious to beat the Cup-h3lders. It is, to my mind, questionable whether the same team can go through the season with success. They are not over-strong on the wing, and so far there does not seem much likelihood of a new "flier " being netted. No one recognises more fully than the mem- bers of the Committee that this is the weak spot in the team and every effort will be made to secure a speedy player to ring the changes with Brooks and Fish.

The first practice match was held on Saturday afternoon, and over 1,000 spectators attended. Twenty-four players turned out, and most of them showed capital form. Another practice was held on Tuesday evening, so that by September 7th the men should all be fit, and well able to account for Leigh. Among those who have so far given evidetiae of being in form are Taylor, Dickenson, Isherwood, Brooks, Lees, Hockenhall, Cartwright and Lambert, while the forwards generally seam frisky. By, the way, Jack Preston is turning out again, and he certainly looks in better condition than he was last season. Fish is, as usual, too fat and will need some hard work before he is fit for the first team. Jenkins has not been seen so far, but will probably be on the spot when required.

There will be another practice match at 6 o'clock this evening. Admission, id. The proceeds go to the Infirmary.

The Football Club Smoking Concert for members of the Club, to celebrate the Cup victory last April, will be held in Parr Hall, on Tuesday, September 3rd.

Cricket.

St. Helens must be clngrattfatei upon securing the championship of the West Lancashire League, although one naturally sympathises with Warrington in again losing the honour after a plucky fight. Still the Saints have worked hard to attain their proud position, and are entitled to full praise. Last Saturday Warrington played their concluding league match of the season, and registered a victory at the expense of Stanley. Going in first Warrington compiled 108, W. Richardscn dis- tinguishing himself with a grand display, and knocking no a faultless 29. The Stanley men were all dismissed for 77, R. W. holding the scores with 28. W. H. Podmore was in great form with the ball, and accounted for seven wickets at the small cost of 21 runs.

SECOND DIVISIOX.

Earlestown Warrington Prescot St. Helens Recs. St. Helens Newton Stanley

Warrington 2nd had a very easy task in disposing of Stanley 2nd who could only place five men in the field. The visitors suffered an ignominious defeat, all being out for 39 to which Warrington responded with 149. Alfred Thesvlis, the ex-professional, batted brilliantly and placed 5(3 to his credit.

in the Warrington and District League encounters, the chief feature was the defeat of St. James', who at present occupy the top place, by Cheshire Lines engineers. The Latchford men gave a very poor exhibi- tion, for out of the 17 runs obtained C. Holt scored 10, and there were no fewer than seven "ducks," Riley (12) was the top scorer for the victors. J. cKay bowled remarkably well, capturing olght wickets for seven VMS.

- - , The contest for the championship is becoming dis- tinctly interesting and St. James' will have to be on their mettle if they moan to carry off the gold medals.

Wilderspool Brewery accounted for their rivals from Walker's Brewery. Edwards taking five wickets for exactly as many runs. Orford Barracks triumphed over Bewsey Wesleyaus and Mackie's defeated Castle Rubber.

The Works Competition is nearing its close. The semi-final between Nfackie's and Fletcher's aroused groat interest, and the printers suffered an unexpected rebuff. Fletcher's were very smart in their fielding—a depart- ment in which Nfackie's were not sufficiently wide- awake. It must be said for the losers that they were troubled by the bad light. Carter and Ratcliffe wrought great havoc among the stumps, taking four wickets for ten runs and two for two respectively. J. Burrows (31) was the most successful batsman. Burrell, for the printers, did exceedingly well with the ball, taking six wickets for 19 runs.

Sankey Wire Mills found High Lqgh too hard a nut to crack, the villagers being victorious to the extent of 27. Gleave bowled in capital style, seven wickets falling for 27 runs.

Orford had difficulty in trouncing Sankey, the feature of the game being the splendid batting of Lawton who made 33 not out.

Grappenhcill 2nd entertained Btrton and gained the victory, thanks in a great measure to the bstting of Callinder, Slack, and Peters who added runs at a time when they were most needed.

Walker's Brewery gained the honour of being the first champions of the Manchester and District Brewery Cricket League by defeating Cornbrook Brewery at Arpley on Saturday. For the winners H. Cook, J. Darton and J. Dugan batted soundly. The Dargan- Norman combination was again too strong for the opposition 4 wickets for 8 runs and 5 forl2 respectively being the harvest on this occasion.

There will be a great attraction at St. Helens to-day, when the champions will meet the following team representing the League :—;1. W. Bibby (captain), J. Cresswell, T. Wright (Warrington), H. PULPIT REFERENCE.

Preaching at the Parish Church, on Sunday evening, the Rev. T. W. Barber sAid they had all sustained a great loss by Mr. Willcock's removal, and he could not allow that occasion to pass without referring to it. His death was a sad loss to the cause of education as well as to the town in general. All who had had the pleasure of knowing Mr. Willcocks intimately would cheerfully admit that he was the personification of Christian kindness. He was a man amongst men—not "one of the best," but the best of the best. The ways of God were inscrutable, and past finding out, and as they bowed their beads to the dictum of Providence they prayed that sustaining grace might be granted to the members of the stricken family in the hour of their bereavement.

BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON. -- A Word to the Children.

My dear Beautiful Warrington children,—l've been wondering if you would not like to hear something about tbis dear old town of ours, which you are trying to make beautiful, clean and tidy ; but I'm thinking this is not yet the aim of every boy and girl in War- rington, because I notice that many walls are disfigured with chalk markings and the streets are still littered with paper and fruit pee!ings. Perhaps the culprits are the tiny tots who are not old enough to attend school and hear about our Beautiful Society. Will the older boys and girls toll the little ones about what they are trying to do for our town ? Then the little ones will not spoil the walls by making ugly chalk marks on them and they can put their toffee papers in the waste paper baskets.

You know, in the country, there are no waste paper baskets placed in the lanes. I have been spending my holidays in a very quiet country place. One day,when was taking a walk down a beautiful lane, I saw in the distance, a very nice old gentleman standing by a bon- fire of paper. I thought it was only naughty little boys who played with fire, but here was a nicely- dressed gentleman, very seriously stirring the paper together with his walking-stick, while his pretty brown doggie sat watching him. I supnose I must have looked very surprised when I reached the gentleman, for he turned to me and said with a smile, "I am destroying the paper which makes the lane so untidy." You see, children, some thoughtless people had thrown the paper in the lane, and the old gentleman saw how it was spoiling the beauty of the lane and he was removing the nuisance. Was it not kind of him? Will not you try to follow his example? I don't mean you to burn the paper, because it is dangerous for children to play with fire, but put it in the baskets, which the Beautiful Society has so kindly placed in the streets and playgrounds. I was going to tell you something about our old town, but I think this letter is long enough so I must leave it for another time.

I hope all who can, will pay a visit to the Flower Show and if you have not a plant to bring to the exhibition this time, gather a bunch of wild flowers, arrange them very prettily and see if you cannot win a prize.—I am, deir children, yours affectionately.

V.M.T.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church.

A revival mission is being conducted in the above church by the Rev. W. R. Bird. It commenced on Sunday morning, and will be concluded on Monday evening by the holding of a tea meeting. The services on Sunday were particularly successful, the church being crowded in the evening, and there bad been good at tsndances during the week. There is reason to believe that the mission is being successful.

THE WAY TO A MAN'S HEART.

The old saying that the way to a man's heart is- down his throat may hays been correct at some time; but if he suffers from his liver no amount of good meals - will make him more lovable or cheerful. At this time of the year the liver is very liable to get out of order ; then follows lassitude, headache, shortness of temper. An eminent authority prescribes: Tincture of Buchu, one ounce; Karox Compound, one ounce; Syrup of Lemon, two ounces; which, if taken in doses of a tea- spoonful after each meal and at bedtime, is said to correct the trouble quickly. Any chemist keeps these ingredients, and will make the prescription up, or it may be mixed at home. This simple remedy also has a peculiar healing and soothing effect upon the entire Kidney and Urinary structure, often overcoming the worst forms of back- ache and bladder weakness in juat a little while. This mixture is said to remove all blood disorders and relieve, Rheumatism. Our readers should try it for a week and. note result.
